#5865. The Top 10 Reasons to Be Thankful in 2026

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: The Top 10 Reasons to Be Thankful in 2026

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

When the world is looking especially bleak—as it was after Election Day in 2024—making a list of the things I’m thankful for is a challenge. But that isn’t the case this year. Although the state of the world in 2026 contains much to regret and many things to worry about, there are some salient developments that have me feeling especially grateful as I contemplate the upcoming holiday. Without further ado, here are the top 10 things that I’m thankful for this year.

1. The U.S. Midterms. It turns out U.S. democracy is healthier than many people thought—which is not to say that it doesn’t need some serious improvements—and the Trump administration was as inept at rigging things as many of us had hoped. And make no mistake: This election was far more than the usual setback that incumbent parties face in the midterms. It was a drubbing of historic proportions. It took real political ineptitude to turn Texas and South Carolina blue, for example, and to produce a nine-seat swing in the Senate. Full points to the successful Democratic candidates and to party stalwarts like Stacey Abrams who laid much of the groundwork, but you have to give Trump and his minions credit for enacting policies that a clear majority of Americans really hated. Because I like the rule of law, the separation of powers, a competent and noncorrupt government, disciplined policy-making, separation of church and state, and other quaint traditions, I’m grateful that most of my fellow citizens agree (along with a dislike for foolish wars, cockeyed trade policies, predatory hegemony, oligarchic excess, sexual misconduct, rising living costs, and deliberate and probably illegal efforts to gut public services).

When the world is looking especially bleak—as it was after Election Day in 2024—making a list of the things I’m thankful for is a challenge. But that isn’t the case this year. Although the state of the world in 2026 contains much to regret and many things to worry about, there are some salient developments that have me feeling especially grateful as I contemplate the upcoming holiday. Without further ado, here are the top 10 things that I’m thankful for this year.

1. The U.S. Midterms. It turns out U.S. democracy is healthier than many people thought—which is not to say that it doesn’t need some serious improvements—and the Trump administration was as inept at rigging things as many of us had hoped. And make no mistake: This election was far more than the usual setback that incumbent parties face in the midterms. It was a drubbing of historic proportions. It took real political ineptitude to turn Texas and South Carolina blue, for example, and to produce a nine-seat swing in the Senate. Full points to the successful Democratic candidates and to party stalwarts like Stacey Abrams who laid much of the groundwork, but you have to give Trump and his minions credit for enacting policies that a clear majority of Americans really hated. Because I like the rule of law, the separation of powers, a competent and noncorrupt government, disciplined policy-making, separation of church and state, and other quaint traditions, I’m grateful that most of my fellow citizens agree (along with a dislike for foolish wars, cockeyed trade policies, predatory hegemony, oligarchic excess, sexual misconduct, rising living costs, and deliberate and probably illegal efforts to gut public services).

2. The Global Backlash Against Autocracy. Here’s something more to celebrate: This trend wasn’t just confined to the United States. I’m also thankful that voters in Brazil, Hungary, and Serbia made similar choices, rejecting would-be (or in some cases, incumbent) autocrats and their self-dealing schemes. To be sure, strongman rule continues in many places, and there is reason to worry about the upcoming election in France, the enduring popularity of right-wing parties in Germany, and the authoritarian trends that have returned to parts of Latin America. But I’ll give thanks that what looked like a cresting autocratic wave has been halted, at least for now.

3. Being a Jerk Lost Its Political Appeal. There is one final aspect of the political landscape that deserves a separate mention: The apparent rejection of asshole-ry in the body politic and a renewed desire for principled but civil behavior by political leaders. In recent decades, acting like a complete jerk—lying, engaging in vulgar name-calling, embracing misogyny and racism, trolling relentlessly, and such—had been a surprisingly effective political tactic. U.S. President Donald Trump is the leading example, of course, but a similar approach was embraced by the likes of Pete Hegseth, Vivek Ramaswamy, Laura Loomer, Ken Paxton, Lauren Boebert, Nancy Mace, and Greg Abbott, among others. It was part of the standard playbook of Fox News and provocateurs like Ben Shapiro. By 2026, in fact, acting like a jerk had become a central part of the MAGA/GOP brand. This approach was successful in part because it made Republicans look and sound like real fighters (as when Trump told a rally crowd that “I am your vengeance”) at a moment when mainstream Democrats seemed unclear about what they believed and terminally hamstrung by wokeness.

But over the past year, Democrats produced a bunch of candidates who made it clear that they had strong beliefs that they were willing to stand up for, even if their views were controversial. But at the same time, people like Zohran Mamdani, James Talarico, Abdul el-Sayed, Annie Andrews, and Jon Ossoff come across as likeable folks who want to focus on policies rather than personalities, and they don’t engage in a lot of nasty personal attacks. Notably, Democrats dumped toxic candidates such as Graham Plattner in Maine, while Republicans (and especially Trump) doubled down on some of theirs. Surprise, surprise: It turned out that American voters have grown tired (if not disgusted) by political figures are chronic liars, selfish, corrupt, and generally mean-spirited jerks, especially when they are also incompetent. One may therefore hope for the return of civility to American public discourse, which would help heal some of the country’s current divisions. And as one watches the British electorate sour on faux-populist Nigel Farage, one may even hope that this trend extends beyond the United States.

4. Balancing Behavior. I ’ll confess to a certain vested interest here, but I’ve been pleasantly struck by evidence of balancing behavior in a wide variety of contexts. Balance-of-power politics often gets a bad rap, as a world of competing powers is seen as one that exacerbates tensions and eventually leads to greater conflict. But as we saw during the unipolar era, unchecked power can be equally (if not more) dangerous and result in a bunch of pointless wars. One of the many problems with “liberal hegemony” is that it made global security overly dependent on U.S. engagements almost everywhere, which made peace dependent on Washington showing good judgment and consistently doing the right thing. Even before the arrival of Trump, there were good reasons to question that assumption.

If states balance readily against potential threats, and if leaders recognize that this is the case, then states that might be tempted to challenge the status quo will be deterred by the prospect of facing combined opposition. The knowledge that others will also face similar constraints also reduces every state’s need to make gains to be secure. Thus, efficient balancing behavior can be a powerful cause of peace, as it was during the Cold War.

For this reason, I’m heartened by the evidence of renewed balancing, even if it is currently less efficient than one might wish. Europe is rearming after years of neglect, Middle Eastern states are forging new arrangements that are less dependent on unilateral U.S. protection, and Asian countries continue to coordinate responses to rising Chinese power and assertiveness. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney’s call for joint efforts to counter the Trump administration’s predatory policies is consistent with this logic, as well. Let us hope that the next U.S. administration recognizes the danger of provoking ever-growing resistance, rediscovers the value of constructive ties with allies, and adjusts its behavior accordingly.

5. The Gulf on Israel Widens and Deepens. Last year, I expressed some thanks that U.S. public attitudes toward Israel were shifting, and that trend has accelerated over the past year. The electoral victories by candidates who were openly critical of Israel’s behavior is an obvious sign of this development, as is Senator Chris Van Hollen’s challenge to Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer’s efforts to become majority leader next year. Van Hollen may fall short, but the fact that he could pose as a credible alternative is evidence that we are living in a very different world.

My thanks that the American body politic is shifting to a more defensible position is tempered by my fear that it is too little and much too late. Had this shift occurred a couple of decades ago, it might have salvaged the two-state solution, marginalized Hamas politically, bolstered liberal forces within Israel and halted its own spiral toward extremism, opened the door to gradual détente with Iran, and left the Middle East far better off than it is today. While I’m thankful that the United States is finally coming to its senses on this issue, I cannot look upon the current situation with any sense of pleasure.

6. Tech Bros Brought Down to Earth. A year ago, tech bros like Elon Musk, Mark Zuckerberg, Sam Altman, Peter Theil, Jensen Huang, and others were riding high. Not only were they amassing unreal amounts of wealth, but they were pouring millions of dollars into the political system and performing end runs around efforts to rein them in.

I don’t want to overstate this trend—the latest developments at Nvidia underscore that tech leaders still have the ear of the president—but I’m glad to see public opinion and public officials begin to demand greater accountability from these self-designated masters of the universe. There’s a growing pushback against data centers, Musk’s millions didn’t prevent the blue tsunami, the saga of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang has also exposed potentially corrupt dealings by U.S. officials and revealed that some of the corporate titans care more about their own bank accounts than they do about national security. Looking ahead, it seems likely that politicians in the United States, Europe, and elsewhere are going to start regulating artificial intelligence. It’s about time.

Yes, I know: Huang has been reinstated at Nvidia, SpaceX and Anthropic had record-setting IPOs, and all these companies will likely play an outsized role in the economy and in politics for years to come. But for now, I’ll be thankful that some of their hubris has been checked. Heck, even Musk is now trying to deny that he was ever MAGA—despite the abundant evidence to the contrary. Either he’s lying or he has no principles at all—there’s no third option.

7. Dogs That Didn’t Bark. As I said at the outset, there’s no shortage of things to worry about if you’re looking for reasons not to feel thankful. Efforts to reduce climate change are failing; global public health issues are rising; the wars in Ukraine, Sudan, the Persian Gulf, etc., are still raging and causing vast suffering; measles is back in the United States (give thanks to the Trump administration and especially U.S. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. for that dubious “achievement”); and we are all wondering where the AI revolution will take us. If you want to have a gloomy Thanksgiving, therefore, that option is available.

Nonetheless, we should also pause and reflect on the bad things that could have happened but didn’t. The world economy didn’t suffer a new financial panic or depression. We didn’t face another global pandemic. Humanity marked the 81st consecutive year since Hiroshima in which nuclear weapons were not used and none of the great powers fought each other. The European Union didn’t collapse, the United States didn’t invade Greenland, China didn’t blockade or attack Taiwan, and the war in the Persian Gulf didn’t escalate in truly catastrophic fashion (e.g. large-scale attacks on desalinization plants).

8. Guilty (and Not So Guilty) Pleasures. As usual, I’m grateful for the people who educated, amused, inspired, and entertained me over the years. I enjoyed nonfiction works by Eli Clifton and Ian Lustick, Stephen Kinzer, Chad Levinson, Kimmo Rentola, and Daniel Walker Howe, as well the commentaries by David French, Paul Krugman, Lydia Polgreen, David Rothkopf, and most of the regular columnists at the Financial Times. I don’t get to read as many novels as I’d like, but Colson Whitehead, Alessandra Ranelli, Curtis Sittenfeld, Barbara Trapido, and a few others brought me a lot of enjoyment this past year. I never grow tired of rereading Raymond Chandler or Rex Stout, and I finally got around to reading Ursula K. Le Guin’s The Left Hand of Darkness, a mere 57 years after it was first published. My wife and I tapped our feet to big band jazz at Birdland, enjoyed Don Cheadle and Ayo Edebiri in Proof, and were blown away by Paul Giamatti’s performance in Eugène Ionesco’s Rhinoceros at the American Repertory Theater. The Red Sox resurgence fell short in the playoffs but their record-setting winning streak was great fun. The Yankees didn’t make the World Series, and this lifelong Dodgers fan found himself rooting for the Brewers instead. (I mean, seriously, how could one not?) And given my earlier predictions, it’s hard not to feel a bit smug about the demise of the Saudi-sponsored LIV Golf tour. I don’t watch much TV apart from sports, but I’m thankful they brought Ted Lasso back for one more season (but really, enough is enough), and I hope Apple TV renews Sugar for a third season.

9. The Golden Age of Academia. This item is bittersweet, as the world in which I have spent almost all my career is slowly vanishing. I feel enormous gratitude for having been part of it, yet I am filled with apprehension and sadness at its present trajectory. I’ve been a full-time academic for the better part of 50 years, and whatever success I have enjoyed is only partly due to my own efforts. In addition to a whole lot of lucky breaks, I was fortunate to be part of a golden era in American higher education, when society placed a high value on research and teaching, professors were generally respected, and both state and federal officials understood that a well-funded, unconstrained, and diverse academic community made the whole country stronger and wealthier.

Sadly, that world is now imperiled. A small part of the problem lies within higher education itself (excessive bureaucratization, overemphasis on intercollegiate sports, self-imposed limits on free inquiry, reluctance to police genuine misconduct, etc.), and the emergence of AI is almost certain to transform the entire enterprise in ways that are still hard to predict. But most of the trouble arises from a concerted attack on universities from the political right, a campaign that began long before Trump but has been supercharged under him. In addition to defunding key lines of research and damaging institutions once universally acknowledged as the world’s best (and a magnet for talented young people from all over the world), government efforts to impose ideological conformity on universities guarantees that the brightest minds will pursue other careers, important questions will receive inadequate attention, truth will get censored, and society will know less and misunderstand more. I remain deeply thankful for the life that I’ve been privileged to lead, and I’m alarmed by the obstacles that current and future scholars will face.

10. A Personal Note. I’ll spend a fair bit of the next three weeks reviewing the page proofs for a new book, which means I can spend Thanksgiving Day giving thanks that the damn thing is finished. The title is The Five Keys to a Safer World: A Foreign Policy Primer, and it will be published by Princeton University in late spring 2027. I’m immensely grateful to the institutions and individuals who made it possible (you can read about them in the acknowledgements), but I’m also greatly relieved that I’m not still writing it. I hope each of you can look back on some important achievement or milestone in 2026 and look forward to 2027 with anticipation and hope.

Stephen M. Walt is a columnist at Foreign Policy and the Robert and Renée Belfer professor of international relations at Harvard University. Walt is the author of The New Geopolitics, a five-part newsletter masterclass breaking down the biggest concepts you should use to navigate today’s world. #5861. The Global Antiauthoritarian Wave Is an Illusion

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

LARRY M. BARTELS is University Distinguished Professor of Political Science and Law and May Werthan Shayne Chair of Public Policy and Social Science at Vanderbilt University.

Democracy Can’t Be Saved at the Ballot Box

In 2022, many observers celebrated the results of the United States’ midterm elections as a victory for democracy. The Republican Party underperformed at the ballot box, and some of the candidates most outspoken in support of former President Donald Trump’s historic effort to overturn the 2020 election outcome were among the casualties. President Joe Biden concluded that the American people “have proven once again that democracy is who we are.” Reporters for The New York Times concurred, writing that the midterm outcome demonstrated “the resilience of the country’s democracy” as the electorate rejected “openly extremist Republican candidates.”

But that rosy interpretation was more the result of wishful thinking than careful analysis. Most of the Republicans who lost were simply bad, inexperienced candidates. In fact, Republican members of Congress who had flouted the democratic process by voting to decertify electoral votes on January 6, 2021, suffered no discernible penalty at the polls, while the minority who had stood up for the rule of law faced stiff backlash from their party’s establishment and from primary voters. And, of course, two years later, Trump stormed back into the White House.

In this year’s midterms, Trump’s party took another, even bigger beating. And again, there has been a rush to celebrate the result as a triumph for democratic values. NPR reported that “one of the big, clear winners of the midterms is American democracy itself.” Amanda Taub, an international affairs columnist at The New York Times, upped the ante, writing that “An anti-authoritarian wave appears to be crashing over elections around the world.” Just as in 2022, however, there is reason to fear that this optimistic account seriously misconstrues what happened at the polls on November 3 and what the result means for the future. History shows that voters in the United States, as in other democracies, are much more focused on the competence of their political leaders and their own well-being than on adherence to democratic procedures. To imagine that they have suddenly become guardians of democratic norms is to misunderstand the electorate’s role in the democratic process—and to underestimate the chronic susceptibility of the American political system to democratic backsliding.

It is natural to “feel pride,” as the New York Times editorial board put it, in the notion that ordinary citizens “stood up” for democratic principles at the ballot box. Democracy is the United States’ civil religion, and the voice of the people is the closest thing Americans have to the voice of God. But as the British jurist and historian Henry Sumner Maine observed in 1885, the oracle of democracy is often “not as intelligible as might be desired.” Electoral behavior is complex, and interpretations of election outcomes constructed in the hours and days after a vote often turn out to be far off the mark. Voters in democracies are less ideological than analysts generally assume. They are also less committed to abstract democratic norms than high-minded observers might hope. In a 2020 study, the Yale political scientists Matthew Graham and Milan Svolik used survey experiments to test the impact of democratic norm violations on Americans’ support for hypothetical candidates. They concluded that “only a small fraction of Americans prioritize democratic principles in their electoral choices when doing so goes against their partisan identification or favorite policies.” In 2022, real voters demonstrated the same point with their willingness to support Republican members of Congress who had participated in Trump’s bogus “stop the steal” effort.

This year, the exit polls run by a consortium of American news organizations provided superficial support for the notion that the midterm “blue wave” was an explicit rejection of Trump’s continuing assault on democratic norms. These surveys asked voters which of six issues was the most important facing the country. A third of respondents chose “the state of democracy,” and an overwhelming proportion of those who did, 79 percent, voted for Democratic congressional candidates. Taken at face value, these data suggest that more than a quarter of the electorate voted Democratic because they opposed Trump’s efforts to subvert American democracy.

Alas, people’s explanations for their own behavior are often misleading. Responses to list choices of this sort are particularly sensitive to the specific options presented and their favorable or unfavorable connotations. Given the choice, most people would probably prefer to attribute their votes to principled support for democracy than to pique at the price of gas. But when a separate YouGov poll conducted just after the vote asked an open-ended version of the same question, only five percent of voters spontaneously cited government corruption or the undermining of democracy as the most important factor motivating their choice.

A closer look at the exit poll data offers additional grounds for skepticism regarding the importance of democratic principles for the 2026 midterm outcome. The largest swing away from the Republican Party, more than 30 points, was among young voters—who are generally much less supportive of democracy than other age cohorts. (A 2025 Kettering Foundation survey, for instance, found that although 89 percent of Silent Generation respondents agreed with the proposition that “democracy is the best form of government,” only 54 percent of Gen Z respondents did.) To suppose that young people are now the vanguard of a sudden urge to defend democracy is implausible.

Finally, a comparison between the 2026 and 2024 exit polls is telling. In 2022, well before the depredations of Trump’s second term, voters were presented a similar list choice of issues that “mattered most in deciding how you voted for president.” A similar share of voters cited “the state of democracy” that year as did this year, and a similar overwhelming majority of them reported voting for Kamala Harris. The key difference between the two elections was not in the importance that voters assigned to democratic principles but in the impact of economic grievances. In 2024, 32 percent of exit poll respondents said the economy mattered most for their vote, and 81 percent of those respondents reported voting for Trump. In 2026, 39 percent said that the economy was the most important issue facing the country—and most of them voted for Democrats.

What about the notion that “an anti-authoritarian wave appears to be crashing over elections around the world”? As with many “waves” identified by political analysts, this antiauthoritarian trend mostly amounts to a short list of cases in which autocrats (or would-be autocrats) lost elections. Taub, the New York Times commentator, pointed to Brazil, Hungary, and Serbia, along with the U.S. midterm elections, as evidence for a wave. But it is a stretch to see antiauthoritarian sentiment as the common thread explaining these outcomes.

In Brazil in October, the incumbent president, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva (known as Lula), defeated Flávio Bolsonaro, the son of his right-wing populist predecessor, by less than four percentage points. The result was unremarkable, except for the fact that Bolsonaro had led Lula in the first round of voting. The most salient event separating the two votes was a televised debate in which Bolsonaro seemed to mock Lula for having lost a finger in a factory accident. “I was a minor and working at night,” Lula responded, “because either I worked or I did not eat. I will not accept a posh guy calling me ‘nine-fingers.’ ” It was a dramatic moment, but hardly a test of Brazilian voters’ commitment to democracy.

The April election in Hungary provides a clearer parallel with the U.S. midterm race. Viktor Orban’s Fidesz party was defeated after 16 years of autocratic rule—but Hungarians’ attitudes toward democracy likely played a smaller role in both Orban’s rise and his fall than many observers imagine. His triumph in 2010 was widely but erroneously portrayed as part of a global populist wave. In fact, data from the European Social Survey (ESS) show that his supporters were not more distrustful of politicians, more dissatisfied with democracy, more opposed to immigration or the EU, or more eager for order and tradition than other Hungarian voters. What they were, mostly, was fed up with their incumbent government.

Orban used his sweeping election victory as a pretext to substantially erode Hungarian democracy, pressuring judges and the press and fiddling with election rules to entrench Fidesz’s dominance. Those moves no doubt contributed to his long tenure in office. But the more important factor was that, from 2009 to 2019, a series of ESS surveys recorded substantial increases in ordinary Hungarians’ satisfaction with their economy, their government, their lives as a whole, and, ironically, even “the way democracy works in Hungary.” When that sense of well-being faded, Orban’s autocratic machinations proved unable to protect him. As the Christian Science Monitor reporter Mark Sappenfield put it, “The story of his political demise is, in the end, not so different from countless democratic elections everywhere. The economy stagnated, highlighting levels of corruption that became intolerable to voters, who then lost trust in Mr. Orbán’s ability to guide them to better lives.”

Much the same could be said of Trump in 2026. One of the oldest and best-supported generalizations in political science is the law of the pendulum. It asserts that the political costs of governing—stemming from, for example, ideological overreach, complacency, scandals, or economic shocks—tend to erode support for incumbent political leaders and their parties, resulting in the restoration of competitive balance and periodic alteration of parties in power. Of course, these political costs do not accumulate with anything like the uniformity implied by the image of a pendulum swinging consistently from left to right and back again. Some governments are beset by problems and succumb in short order, while others face smoother seas and survive—for a time. But no democratic government lasts forever.

When incumbents use state resources—sometimes including legal and physical intimidation—to entrench themselves in power, it may feel like the law of the pendulum has been suspended. Nonetheless, even the most dominant political machines succumb eventually to hard times, economic and social change, internal divisions, and mounting pressure from those excluded from power. As the Vanderbilt political scientist Jessica Trounstine noted in a 2008 study of the rise and fall of political “bosses” in U.S. cities, the result may be chaotic: “In many cases the fall of monopolies set in motion periods of intense factionalism during which gridlock was common.” Turnover may result in protracted turmoil or in the emergence of a new machine, perhaps under the banner of reform. The fall of a strongman or boss does not automatically produce a well-functioning democratic system.

If characterizing the midterm elections as a triumph for democratic norms misconstrues why voters made their choices, it also risks misrepresenting what comes next. The New York Times editorial board envisioned quick progress to halt democratic backsliding: “The outcome creates a crucial check on Mr. Trump’s autocratic behavior. . . . The country already feels changed, and it will in fact be changed in January.”

Changed, certainly; but the president and his allies are not going anywhere, and they are far from powerless. A glance around the world provides examples of the intense factionalism and gridlock that may be in store for the United States during the next two years. In Poland, an autocratic government fell in 2023 after eight years in power. But the holdover president, Karol Nawrocki, has battled Prime Minister Donald Tusk’s institutional reform efforts. Tusk’s attempt to remake the constitutional court—packed with friends of the former regime—precipitated a constitutional crisis pitting the prime minister and the EU against the president and mobs of right-wing protesters. Tusk’s election was just one step in what will be a long struggle to restore Poland’s liberal democracy.

The American president is much more powerful than Poland’s, providing much more scope for bitter political conflict over policies and democratic procedures. In one election-night interview, Trump claimed that he would “look forward to working with” the victorious Democrats. But earlier in the day, to CBS News’s Robert Costa, he noted his victory in the Supreme Court case Trump v. Slaughter (which upheld his right to fire a Federal Trade Commission member) and claimed that “the Slaughter case gives the president tremendous powers that it never had before.” Another person Costa described as “close to the president” told him that “while Democrats may win” the election, the president would “keep the power to do almost everything he wants.” The post-election appointment of Stephen Miller as Trump’s chief of staff does not bode well for cooperation.

Of course, Democrats hope to win back the White House in 2028. But even if they do, it would be naive to expect Trump’s legacy of democratic backsliding to be erased. He has done substantial, durable harm to the federal government and the American political system. Some of his most egregious procedural and policy changes will be rolled back, but only by diverting attention and political capital from new priorities. Others will be harder to undo. Millions of years’ worth of governing experience cast aside in Elon Musk’s ham-handed purge of the civil service will not be restored with the stroke of a pen. The massive expansion of the Immigration and Customs Enforcement agency, its extensive network of detention facilities, and its culture—which the legal scholar L. Ali Khan described as “performative cruelty”—will not be quickly or easily dismantled. The Supreme Court will not revert to the ideological mainstream any time soon.

Perhaps the most significant damage of the Trump era has been to long-standing, informal democratic norms that supposedly constrained the behavior of political leaders. Observers who expected Trump to pay a stiff political price for his abusive rhetoric, incessant lying, self-dealing, and disdain for court rulings, Congress, foreign allies, and the electoral process have learned that, as the University of Denver political scientist Seth Masket put it, “a lot of the things we think voters just won’t tolerate turn out to be tolerable to them.” That lesson, once learned, is very hard to unlearn. If future political leaders refrain from these behaviors, it will be because they want to, not because they think they have to.

Trump’s trashing of political norms accelerated a process that was already underway before he turned his sights on the White House. In 2012, the veteran political analysts Thomas Mann and Norman Ornstein argued that hyperpartisanship was eroding the compromise and self-restraint necessary to facilitate power sharing in the United States’ peculiarly decentralized national government. The logic of partisan tit for tat has encouraged each party to respond to the other’s norm violations by pushing even more aggressively to weaponize the filibuster, bypass Congress with executive orders, and gerrymander congressional district boundaries. Each successful norm violation has encouraged further probing of legal and political redlines.

Hyperpartisanship will not disappear when Trump departs the political scene; he is its consequence more than its cause. Most important, the passion that has fueled the Make America Great Again movement will not vanish. Millions of Americans still see the waves of social change that have washed over the United States since the 1960s as a cultural disaster, and the looming prospect of a majority-minority country as a threat to be battled, by undemocratic means if necessary. They will find new champions.

A survey commissioned by NPR and PBS to mark the United States’ 250th birthday found that 82 percent of Americans agreed that “the issues that divide the nation” constitute “a serious threat to the future of our democracy.” If that seems alarmist, it is worth noting that 37 percent of the same respondents in the same survey—and 58 percent of those under the age of 30—agreed that “Americans may have to resort to violence in order to get the country back on track.” When people view political conflict in existential terms, they are unlikely to feel constrained by high-minded democratic theory.

#5860. Latin America’s ‘Orange Wave’ Is Not Spurning China

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Latin America’s ‘Orange Wave’ Is Not Spurning China

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

In Argentina, a giant Chinese-backed telescope project at the base of the Andes has sat partly built for more than a year, after authorities paused construction in mid-2025. In February, the Panamanian government ejected a subsidiary of a Hong Kong-based company from two ports on either side of the Panama Canal. And Mexico has imposed tariffs of up to 50 percent on many Chinese imports since January.

At first glance, these shifts seem to show the success of U.S. President Donald Trump’s aggressive push against Chinese influence in the Americas. His 2025 National Security Strategy pledged to “deny non-Hemispheric competitors” the ability “to own or control strategically vital assets” in the region. Allowing “incursions” by such competitors “without serious pushback is [a] great American strategic mistake of recent decades,” it added.

In Argentina, a giant Chinese-backed telescope project at the base of the Andes has sat partly built for more than a year, after authorities paused construction in mid-2025. In February, the Panamanian government ejected a subsidiary of a Hong Kong-based company from two ports on either side of the Panama Canal. And Mexico has imposed tariffs of up to 50 percent on many Chinese imports since January.

At first glance, these shifts seem to show the success of U.S. President Donald Trump’s aggressive push against Chinese influence in the Americas. His 2025 National Security Strategy pledged to “deny non-Hemispheric competitors” the ability “to own or control strategically vital assets” in the region. Allowing “incursions” by such competitors “without serious pushback is [a] great American strategic mistake of recent decades,” it added.

While Trump has faced resistance to his agenda in Canada and Brazil, much of the hemisphere is led by recently elected right-wing presidents who claim to be allied with Trump. It’s in many of these so-called orange-wave countries—such as Argentina and Panama—that the Trump administration’s warnings about dealings with China have appeared to lead to setbacks for high-profile Chinese investments.

But optics are not the whole story. In many Latin American countries, ties with China have continued to move forward during the Trump years, according to key metrics such as trade flows, visits by high-level officials, and public opinion surveys. Some analysts even argue that the United States’ claim to a sphere of influence in the Americas may be a strategic blunder that could benefit China.

“I think if the Americans choose the path of coercion to try to reach their goals in the region,” said Sebastián Hurtado of Ecuadorian consultancy Prófitas, “it’s going to backfire.”

U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth (right) signs a bilateral agreement with Panama’s President Jose Raul Mulino in Panama City on April 9, 2025. Franco Brana/AFP via Getty Images

Trump’s anti-China push in the Americas has so far targeted—or voiced plans to target—a vast swath of economic activity, including investments in ports, the oil sector, critical minerals, and communications infrastructure. U.S. officials have argued that these assets could be used for Chinese military purposes or that they are needed for U.S. security reasons.

Beijing has retaliated to some of these moves. After Panama’s Supreme Court booted the subsidiary of a Hong Kong-based company out of two ports following Trump’s complaints, China upped maritime detentions of Panama-flagged ships. In Paraguay, China sanctioned a sitting government official for the first time ever: the information technology minister, after Paraguay affirmed ties with Taiwan and blocked Chinese companies from building the country’s 5G network.

Even so, it’s noteworthy that Beijing has chosen a more muted response to other aggressive U.S. moves in the region, experts say. The U.S. apprehension of President Nicolás Maduro of Venezuela—a country that China has dubbed an “all-weather strategic partner”—in January triggered verbal condemnations but no major retaliation. Beijing also showed restraint after Mexico levied steep tariffs on Chinese goods following the United States’ urging.

The country’s troubling relationship to China is at the center of its presidential campaign.

Rather than pushing Beijing out of the region, Washington should offer countries better strategic alternatives.

Even the most MAGA of the region’s leaders can’t abandon their motor of economic development.

On Venezuela, Chinese officials “haven’t made a huge scandal, and they could have,” said Francisco Urdinez, an expert on China-Latin America relations at the Pontifical Catholic University of Chile. Urdinez argued that in general, “China always understood that the hemisphere is a natural sphere of influence for Washington,” adding that the United States “exerts absolute hegemony in military aspects.”

Beijing’s core interests in the region are mostly economic—maintaining access to food and commodities exports and some big-ticket investments—as well as the fact that the region contains the largest share of countries in the world that officially recognize Taiwan, he said.

Many of these economic links are hard to undo because they are governed by long-term contracts. Often, there’s no easy replacement for Chinese buyers. Chile, for example, exports most of its copper and lithium to China rather than the United States in part because China has refining capacity that the United States lacks.

The Trump administration has generally been slow to offer Latin American countries alternatives to doing business with China. Though Ecuadorian President Daniel Noboa is a vocally pro-Trump president, his country’s “trade and investment relationship with the United States is stagnant,” Hurtado said. Chinese trade and investment jumped after a free trade deal took effect in 2024, and Chinese demand has helped boost Ecuador’s shrimp industry to become its top export. Noboa has visited China twice during his current term.

These economic realities help explain why Beijing has sometimes chosen restraint in response to Trump’s so-called Donroe Doctrine. A “couple of wins … lets the administration maybe rest on its laurels a little bit and then get distracted from the broader economic trends that fundamentally haven’t changed,” said Audrye Wong of the American Enterprise Institute.

Brazil’s President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva (left) and China’s Great Wall Motor CEO Mu Feng (right) raise their hands during the inauguration of the GWM automobile factory in Iracemapolis, Brazil on Aug. 15, 2025.Nelson Almeida/AFP via Getty Images

China might have already treated the Americas as a de facto sphere of U.S. military influence before Trump took office. But his administration’s public acknowledgement of the idea has triggered a series of debates in the Chinese, Latin American, and U.S foreign-policy communities over whether China and Latin American countries would welcome explicit spheres of influence.

While right-wing Latin American presidents have gone along with some of Trump’s priorities, especially regarding crime and immigration, they’ve shown unease with full alignment when it comes to China. Many, like Noboa, have continued robust trade with China. Brazil’s newly reelected left-wing president, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, puts things more bluntly: “Dividing the world into zones of influence and making neocolonial incursions for strategic resources are anachronistic moves,” he said at the United Nations General Assembly in September.

In official statements, Beijing says it rejects spheres of influence. But some observers of Chinese foreign policy, including Latin American officials who’ve dealt with Chinese counterparts, have argued that Beijing would be open to the idea—especially if it means the United States staying out of the Asia-Pacific. “China has no problem with the world being divided into spheres of influence” because “it reveals weakness in the idea that the U.S. is the global superpower,” said Hussein Kalout, a former senior Brazilian foreign-policy official.

“I think China would be very satisfied if the world were divided the way it is in Trump’s head,” said Igor Patrick, the Latin America correspondent for the South China Morning Post, based in Rio de Janeiro. “But there’s an issue beyond the geopolitics of military interests, and that’s Chinese investment,” he added.

A construction project at the Port of Balboa, managed by CK Hutchison Holdings based in Hong Kong, at the entrance to the Panama Canal in Panama City on Feb. 23. Martin Bernetti/AFP via Getty Images

Latin American and Caribbean countries have received more than $200 billion in Chinese investment since 2000, according to researchers at the National Autonomous University of Mexico. Many of those investments support the flow of critical minerals back to China, like iron and copper mines, or the global sales strategy for Chinese goods, like electric car factories.

But China’s economic relationships—and its economic influence—are not uniform across the region. Mexico and Central American countries like Panama remain more deeply integrated with the U.S. economy than with China. And it is there that the Trump administration has generally been more successful at pressuring Chinese business to leave. Trump has had less success in Brazil, the world’s top recipient of Chinese investment in 2025.

At a Johns Hopkins University roundtable on Chinese foreign policy earlier this year, multiple speakers brought up another obstacle to a system based on spheres of influence: Beijing does not trust the United States enough to know that any hypothetical deal to respect spheres of influence would endure.

Shiao Wang, an assistant professor at Fudan University who researches Latin America, wrote in an email that she “would be cautious about interpreting China’s responses to particular U.S. initiatives as evidence that a particular geopolitical arrangement was accepted.” She added that Latin American countries’ own economic needs help explain China’s relations in the region.

For now, rather than being traded as part of a grand bargain between the United States and China, Latin America finds itself in the middle of an uncomfortable tug-of-war.

Demonstrators during an anti-Trump protest in Bogotá, Colombia, on Jan. 7.Andres Rot/Getty Images

It’s unclear how much Chinese business Trump will manage to push out of Latin America during his term. But one trend is emerging: Throughout the region, public opinion of China is improving relative to that of the United States.

Earlier this year, respondents to a Pew poll in five out of six Latin American countries said that their views of the United States had worsened. Views of China largely remained steady. A Latinobarómetro survey found that since 2024, opinions of China have improved, and opinions of the United States have declined in 15 out of 17 Latin American countries. It was the first time that more people in the region told Latinobarómetro that China was a positive influence than was the United States.

“China is not applying sanctions, going to war, or harming its friends,” Kalout said of the shift, adding that U.S. tariffs and immigration restrictions also took a toll. Urdinez, who studies public opinion about China in the region, cautioned that it has historically been volatile. He said that China’s bump is mainly due to its contrast with Trump’s foreign policy.

Still, he added, China has been attracting some popularity on its own terms through the provision of high-tech goods like BYD cars, which are increasingly common in the region. “Ecuadorians view China as a country that has quickly taken off. And before, we saw the United States like that,” Hurtado said.

For Urdinez, monitoring these public opinion shifts is part of tracking a long-term influence game. Beyond the question of who controls a specific mine or port, he argued that bigger issues are at play—such as whether Latin American countries ultimately align with China’s worldview in global forums like the U.N. If they do, it suggests that China has translated its economic ties into more holistic geopolitical influence in the region, even despite the ubiquity of hard U.S. military assets.

Speaking about Brazil, Kalout said people “prefer the United States over China due to cultural relations, values, economic ties, and related secular traditions.” But whether that relationship proves more durable “depends a lot on how the United States acts in the coming decades,” he said.

Catherine Osborn is the writer of Foreign Policy’s weekly Latin America Brief. She is a print and radio journalist based in Rio de Janeiro. X: @cculbertosborn

#5859. New California law could keep teens in booster seats until age 16 unless they pass test

Why buckling a child into a car seat with heavy winter layers is not recommended.

Beginning in the new year, minors will be required to pass a five-step seat belt fit test before they can ride without a booster seat or sit in the front seat of a vehicle. The bill's author, Assemblymember Lori Wilson, cited figures showing that child injuries and fatalities in crashes have risen since the pandemic, with children ages 8 to 12 disproportionately affected.

"Extending the required age for booster use and implementing straightforward testing can significantly enhance child safety," CalMatters reported.

"So the best thing to do to keep your child safe while riding in a vehicle matches what our law is," Wilson said of the bill in April 2025. "It really is to take into consideration all the varying types of seats that we sit on in the car. This is about safety.

"This is about making sure that if something happens, you are safe, and I think that’s the key thing that we’re trying to drive home is that it is not about treating people like babies."

3 CAR SEAT INSTALLATION MISTAKES YOU NEED TO AVOID

A California law could keep children, including some teenagers, in booster seats until age 16.  (Cars.com)

Previously, children ages 8 and older — or those at least 4 feet, 9 inches tall — could ride without a booster seat. Under the new law, Assembly Bill 435, children ages 8 to 15 may use a regular seat belt without a booster only if they meet all five criteria:

MELATONIN WARNINGS: NEARLY HALF OF PARENTS GIVE IT TO THEIR KIDS TO HELP THEM SLEEP, BUT EXPERTS URGE CAUTION

Side shot on the seats for children mounted in minivan. A new law in Italy requires child car seats to be equipped with alarm devices to prevent parents from leaving children inside vehicles for hours.  (iStock)

Improper car seat and booster use remains widespread, according to federal and industry studies.

A 2011 National Child Restraint Use Special Study found that 46% of car seats and booster seats had at least one major installation or use error that could reduce their effectiveness in a crash.

Nearly all seats had some form of misuse, according to the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration.

A 2021 study by AAA and the National Safety Council found that more than half of all car seats brought to child passenger safety technicians for inspection were improperly installed or used, and that only one in five parents and caregivers sought expert help securing their child's seat.

#5857. FP’s Holiday Book List

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Books: FP’s Holiday Book List

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Picture this: You’re curled up in your favorite chair with a cup of hot cocoa, fire crackling, Bing Crosby crooning on the stereo, and you pull out a 400-plus-page tome on the 1,000-year history of bond markets. Or the adaptability of the European Union. Or perhaps grand strategy in the Middle East. If any of these scenarios appeal to you, you’re in the right place. Below, you’ll find holiday reading recommendations from FP staff and columnists, whose 2026 picks range from wonky to contemplative to downright fun.—Chloe Hadavas

Robin Wigglesworth (Portfolio, 416 pp., $35, September 2026)

Picture this: You’re curled up in your favorite chair with a cup of hot cocoa, fire crackling, Bing Crosby crooning on the stereo, and you pull out a 400-plus-page tome on the 1,000-year history of bond markets. Or the adaptability of the European Union. Or perhaps grand strategy in the Middle East. If any of these scenarios appeal to you, you’re in the right place. Below, you’ll find holiday reading recommendations from FP staff and columnists, whose 2026 picks range from wonky to contemplative to downright fun.—Chloe Hadavas

Robin Wigglesworth (Portfolio, 416 pp., $35, September 2026)

I imagine there are likely only two reactions when readers stumble across a book such as A Fabulous Debt, a 1,000-year tale of bond market magic and mischief: Shoot me now, or straight into my veins. I’m in the latter camp, and unabashedly so. This is a fantastically entertaining read, and no holiday is complete, in our household at least, without in-depth discussions of financial crises dating back centuries.

Robin Wigglesworth’s book is so much fun to read because it is a fast-paced, exhaustively researched history of the driest subject in the world written in the most engaging way possible. Bonds—debt that pays interest, and that finances governments and companies—are actually the secret to understanding the whims and vagaries of everything that has happened since Venice was on the upswing in the pre-Renaissance years to the latest market meltdown over U.S. President Donald Trump’s wild policies. There is literally nothing more central, and perhaps less well understood, to the world today, and Wigglesworth is the worthiest of sherpas.

Beyond all the financial innovation—which was much, and often not even criminal—the characters alone are amazing. From 12th-century Venetian doges to Alexander Hamilton to 19th-century rail barons to 1980s bond villains, and then, just a few decades later, even more creative bond villains. This is the prequel to The Big Short.

If I were a ratings agency, I would give the book an AAA+.

Quinn Slobodian and Ben Tarnoff (Harper, 256 pp., $30, April 2026)

A guide for the perplexed? I feel seen; count me in.

Just as there was once Fordism—the shorthand homage to Henry Ford and 20th-century capitalism that married mass production with mass consumption—our current era of bewilderment probably needed its own -ism. Enter Elon Musk. Co-authors Quinn Slobodian and Ben Tarnoff make the case that Muskism is a worldview that offers an “operating system” for our times. But you, dear reader, are not just partaking in Musk’s OS by buying his cars or renting his internet. You are becoming part of his universe. “Trying to unplug from Musk,” the book points out, “you realize he owns the socket.”

This is a slim, urgent, and highly readable book on the man who thinks he is the world’s most important being. It is also about what Musk’s rise means for the rest of us—the permanently perplexed—whose tax dollars fueled his ambitions, who watched as he gutted the federal government, and who continue to wonder why we’re developing flying cars when all our actual flights are delayed. Mercifully, unlike some other works on Musk, this is no hagiography. Instead, it charts out how Musk came about his views and how his techno-maximalist, anti-humanitarian venom came to poison much of Silicon Valley.

Catherine E. De Vries and Alexandros Kentikelenis (Oxford University Press, 256 pp., $34.99, October 2026)

Who in their right mind would read a book about the European Union during a holiday? Well, think twice. How Europe Survives by Catherine E. De Vries and Alexandros Kentikelenis is one of the more upbeat books you will ever read about the subject. From their perches in Madrid and Milan, the two political scientists explain why the crises that have plagued Europe in the past two decades—such as the eurozone crisis, refugee crisis, Brexit, the war in Ukraine, and the COVID-19 pandemic—have not killed the EU, as many predicted.

In their telling, Europe is like a living mechanism. It improvises and adapts to its circumstances, albeit slowly. With each crisis, Europe’s leaders face a choice: Fall in the precipice or seek compromise. So far, they have opted for the latter. The result is often far from pretty, but as the book reminds us, these compromises generally produce a quality of life, stability, and moderation that make some other nations green with envy.

Emily St. John Mandel (Knopf, 320 pp., $32, September 2026)

It felt like the world was ending most weeks this year. “BREAKING: EVERYTHING” was a pithy summation of that time the planes were grounded and Microsoft Outlook sputtered to a halt. (I’m sure as you read this, fresh hells will have presented themselves.) Amid such systemic collapse, the concept of a dystopic sci-fi novel set in the smoldering ruins of an American civil war was not appealing—until I read a review of Exit Party that said it was “cozy.”

The novel, which begins with a really bad party off the Sunset Strip in the newly formed Republic of California, courses with adrenaline but never resignation. With a dash of the Bourne movies and a soupcon of fellow Canuck Margaret Atwood, Emily St. John Mandel invokes our worst nightmares and then uses them as a mere backdrop to an absorbing story about art, friendship, and love, asking, “Who will you be at the end of the world?”

Wil Haygood (Knopf, 384 pp., $35, February 2026)

By bringing readers deeply into the experiences of African American soldiers during the Vietnam War, Wil Haygood’s The War Within a War confronts us with the reality of the socioeconomic and racial inequality so often at the heart of modern warfare, and especially of its American variants.

Through individual stories that come to life on the page, Haygood documents how Black soldiers were disproportionately represented on the battlefield in Vietnam, and how these soldiers were disproportionately killed and wounded early in the war. Yet they were promoted to positions of command in numbers well beneath those that their presence on the front lines would have suggested. He also shows the indifferent treatment that Black veterans commonly experienced upon their discharge, when many faced few options beyond returning to poverty-ravaged inner-city neighborhoods, where jobs were scarce and cheap narcotics plentiful.

Haygood’s book joins a growing literature about the bitter irony of African American soldiers fighting, from the U.S. Revolution and the Civil War to the present, for the freedom and full citizenship of others, even as they have been denied these things or discriminated against themselves.

Robin Yassin-Kassab (Saqi Books, 352 pp., $27.95, September 2026)

Not long after the Syrian uprising began in 2011, I started receiving email installments of Robin Yassin-Kassab’s blog, Qunfuz. I had never heard of him, but his searing and eloquent takes on Syria ensured that I did not send Qunfuz to my junk folder. So, when I saw that Yassin-Kassab had written a book, it was an easy preorder.

The Blood Between Us is not an eyewitness account. Yassin-Kassab is of Syrian and Scottish descent, lives in the United Kingdom, and only made a few relatively brief forays into Syria during its 14-year conflict. This is actually to the volume’s benefit, however. Yassin-Kassab offers both the intimacy of the bloodletting as told to him by his interlocutors and an analytic detachment that provides perspective on the Syrian revolution as it unfolded.

His central point is that the Syrian people’s triumph in December 2024 was not the bolt from the blue that it seemed. Despite the static battlefield, repression, displacement, and death, the uprising against the Assad regime never ended. Admittedly, The Blood Between Us is not an uplifting holiday read, so maybe hold off for a few weeks—but do not miss it.

Robert Jackson Bennett (Del Rey, 400 pp., $32, August 2026)

The most entertaining book I’ve read this year was Robert Jackson Bennett’s A Trade of Blood, the third in his ongoing series of fantasy detective stories. Bennett’s tales of a cantankerous master detective and her long-suffering, curious sidekick are an exercise in how to keep sequels fresh—and also examinations of the dilemmas of power and the double-sidedness of a government trying to do good things through troublesome means. They can stand with some of the best of Golden Age detective literature, but they also have giant monsters.

Eli Clifton and Ian Lustick (Atria/One Signal Publishers, 304 pp., $29, August 2026)

My holiday recommendation is Eli Clifton and Ian Lustick’s Israel’s Lobby: America in the Grip of a Foreign Power. Israel’s rightward shift, ever-expanding occupation, and Gaza genocide have produced a sea-change in U.S. attitudes toward Israel. Yet Democratic and Republican politicians continue to give Israel nearly unconditional support and diplomatic protection, even when Israel defies U.S. wishes or takes actions that harm U.S. interests. Clifton and Lustick explain this puzzle by showing how hard-line pro-Israel individuals and groups have taken advantage of the lax campaign financing rules created by the 2010 Citizens United decision to keep politicians on board.

Having lost the battle for public opinion, the Israel lobby now relies primarily on raw political muscle, mostly in the form of money. (The recent midterm elections suggest this tactic may be beginning to backfire). Clifton and Lustick also describe how Israel’s most ardent defenders have used false charges of antisemitism to silence criticism of Israel, which poses broader threats to freedom of speech. If you’re curious why the United States keeps landing in quagmires in the Middle East, this book provides part of the answer.

Roger Berkowitz (Yale University Press, 176 pp., $28, October 2026)

At a time when political disagreement can be reason to end a friendship, Roger Berkowitz makes the case that democracy depends on our ability to nurture relationships across deep divides. In A World We Share, Berkowitz, the director of the Hannah Arendt Center at Bard College, offers an anatomy of Arendt’s friendships, which spanned fierce intellectual disagreements and profound moral differences.

Arendt’s circle included figures as disparate as the novelist Mary McCarthy, philosopher Karl Jaspers, and her one-time lover Martin Heidegger, whose embrace of Nazism tested the limits of a friendship with Arendt, a Jewish survivor of Adolf Hitler’s persecution. Berkowitz draws on Arendt’s extensive correspondence, including detailed epistolary exchanges on subjects as diverse as the Holocaust, Watergate, and marital infidelity. Through these relationships, Berkowitz makes the case that democracy depends upon worldviews grounded in mutual respect, spirited debate, and a willingness to break bread, exchange ideas, and forge emotional bonds with people whose convictions we reject.

His argument speaks to a moment when ideological certainties and litmus tests increasingly crowd out curiosity, and political differences can eclipse our ability to see common humanity, much less make common cause. As our politics enters a new and more hopeful phase, and as families and friends gather at holiday tables, this timely book is an invitation to rediscover the democratic necessity of friendship across divides.

C. Thi Nguyen (Penguin Press, 368 pp., $32, January 2026)

The Score, by philosopher C. Thi Nguyen, is a love letter to games, but it is also a surprising treatise on bureaucracy. The book interrogates how so much of modern life has become an exercise of keeping score through data, metrics, and rankings. That focus on the quantitative, Nguyen argues, can produce beauty and fun in a game, yet it can warp behavior to disastrous effect everywhere else.

In public policy, scorekeeping allows governments to create order and track progress in a sprawling, messy world, but often at the expense of context or nuance. Among Nguyen’s case studies is the work of anthropologist Sally Engle Merry on the U.S. State Department’s sex-trafficking report, which, by judging countries by their conviction rates, incentivized greater policing over reducing poverty.

For all its insights on processes and institutions, the book is also a joyous, sensual celebration of rock-climbing, fly-fishing, yo-yoing, and other pursuits that resist the gamification and optimization so characteristic of today’s world.

Tareq Baconi (Washington Square Press, 256 pp., $19, October 2026, paperback)

After I gifted Fire in Every Direction to a friend earlier this year, she sent me a message thanking me for the “novel”; she had read the first few pages and was immediately engrossed. Her misperception speaks to how engagingly Tareq Baconi narrates his own coming-of-age story—it reads like a bildungsroman, but it’s all true.

Baconi is a leading expert on Hamas, which he has previously written about for FP. In Fire in Every Direction, he takes a step back from his academic perch to reflect on his at-times painful journey to self-acceptance as a gay Palestinian man. Since his childhood in Amman, Jordan, Baconi had an intangible sense that he was different from his peers. His unrequited infatuation with a male classmate led to a broader reckoning within his family about sexuality and shame.

To escape Amman’s stifling conservatism, Baconi chose to attend university in London. Although there’s no love lost between him and Jordan—where his twice-displaced Palestinian parents begrudgingly fled to from Beirut during Lebanon’s civil war—he eventually finds his way back to Palestine. Rather than challenge his devotion to the Palestinian cause, Baconi’s queer identity affirms his commitment to securing liberation for all people.

David M. Sacks (Henry Holt and Co., 528 pp., $36.99, November 2026)

The Realist is a lucid, compelling, and granular biography of Hans Morgenthau, one of the most influential scholars of international politics in the immediate postwar era. David M. Sacks’ deft book provides a complete sweep of Morgenthau’s life and career from a small, provincial town in Germany to the global metropolis of New York.

A German-Jewish émigré who had direct experience of the rising tides of vicious antisemitism, Morgenthau succeeded after tireless efforts to find a professorship at the University of Chicago. After an illustrious career, he moved to the City College of New York, where he quickly became a fixture of the city’s intellectual establishment. As a public intellectual, Morgenthau was also an early and vociferous critic of the Vietnam War, despite harboring a deep-seated hostility toward the Soviet Union.

Sonia Gandhi (Knopf, $28, 544 pp., November 2026)

Sonia Gandhi is one of the most fascinating living political leaders in South Asia, but also one of its least understood. She rose from humble beginnings in Italy to become the scion of India’s biggest family dynasty, even becoming linked with India’s independence (her late husband Rajiv was the grandson of the country’s first prime minister, Jawaharlal Nehru). But this remarkable life trajectory also led to great tragedy, due to the assassinations of her mother-in law, Indira, in 1984, and of Rajiv in 1991.

Gandhi, who turns 80 next month, has always been very private about her personal life and rarely opens up publicly. We haven’t had a true sense of her life and how it has shaped her. Belonging, her new memoir released earlier this month, tells a story that many in India and beyond have long wanted to hear.

—Michael Kugelman, FP’s South Asia Brief writer

Saleem Haddad (Europa Editions, 336 pp., $19, February 2026)

One of the most memorable novels I read this year was Saleem Haddad’s Floodlines, a multigenerational epic that stretches from Baghdad to London and elsewhere between. The three Iraqi British sisters at its center—who are variously estranged—are pulled back together by the discovery of lost artwork by their late father, a renowned painter.

The family that Haddad sketches feels real enough that a reader might be tricked into thinking the characters are loosely fictionalized. (The author drew on the story of his own great-uncle, an Iraqi modernist painter, for inspiration.) Even with elements of magical realism at play, Floodlines is ultimately rooted in the political and cultural history of Iraq.

The book reaches its climax in 2014, with the Islamic State on the rise. As an unlikely plot brings its characters back home, Haddad manages to strike a genuine note of hope against a backdrop of conflict, exile, and rupture. And despite the family dysfunction driving it, Floodlines would make an excellent gift for a close relative or friend.

Roger Zakheim (Regnery History, 472 pp., $40, November 2026)

Most accounts of U.S. President Ronald Reagan’s foreign policy focus on his high-level summitry and arms control agreements with Soviet Premier Mikhail Gorbachev. But in Peace Through Strength, Roger Zakheim, the director of the Reagan Institute, tells the story of his military buildup. Zakheim argues that Reagan was able to sustain the largest peacetime military expansion in U.S. history across two presidential terms in the face of stiff opposition, even from his own party, because he saw it as essential to his Cold War strategy.

As the free world faces threats from a new axis of aggressors, Trump calls for a record $1.5 trillion U.S. defense budget, and allied governments in NATO and the Indo-Pacific pledge to spend up to 5 percent of GDP on defense, democratic leaders are once again vexed by the question of how to maintain public and parliamentary support for a yearslong peacetime rearmament program. They would be well served to learn from Reagan’s successful example.

Catherine Liu (Verso, 192 pp., $24.95, September 2026)

I was inspired to read Catherine Liu’s Traumatized after Gregory Jones-Katz’s excellent review of the book in FP—and it was every bit as compelling as promised. Not since Lauren Berlant’s 2011 classic Cruel Optimism have I encountered such a thoughtful treatment of society’s obsession with trauma narratives and the ways they impede our understanding of, and appetite for, political change.

Liu, a cultural theorist at the University of California, Irvine, examines the ways that social media has amplified these narratives, which have come to pervade the political spectrum, contributing to, among other things, inequality, apathy, and the death of liberalism.

It’s not light reading, per se, but it’s provocative, elegantly written, and deeply relevant to our current moment.

Ryan Gingeras (Avid Reader Press / Simon & Schuster, 416 pp., $35, February 2026)

Here is the story of the 20th century told from the other side of the law. In Mafia, historian Ryan Gingeras shows how organized crime made the United States and how the United States in turn remade organized crime on a global scale through its economic might, law enforcement efforts, and popular culture. From early Balkan brigands through the last season of Narcos, this wide-ranging and fast-moving account takes readers on a journey through the dark shadows of globalization.

Mohammed Soliman (Polity, 272 pp., $24.95, February 2026)

In the Washington lexicon, the term “Middle East” often conjures up images of protracted wars, militant groups, and failed U.S. attempts at nation-building. In his new book, analyst Mohammed Soliman argues that moving past these quagmires requires reframing how the region is viewed entirely. Enter “West Asia,” a term that places the region at the edge of the world’s largest continent in lieu of its original Eurocentric conception.

Anchoring his thesis in the evolving ties among the wealthy Arab Gulf states, Israel, India, Egypt, and Japan—to name a few—Soliman argues that Washington should build a new regional order connecting the old Middle East to the Indo-Pacific predicated on realpolitik, technology, and shared security incentives.

A caveat: The book was published weeks before the United States launched its protracted war against Iran, which has injected more uncertainty into the prospect of Soliman’s proposed order. But rather than negating the book’s thesis, the Iran war has only underscored the importance of a new and provocative blueprint for what could come next.

Books are independently selected by FP editors. FP earns an affiliate commission on anything purchased through links to Amazon.com on this page.
