#5856. How Europe Comes Apart

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MUJTABA RAHMAN is Managing Director for Europe at Eurasia Group.

The Four Elections That Could Seal the Continent’s Fate

The European Union is entering a period of political instability that could threaten its future—not through its formal dissolution, but through the gradual erosion of its capacity to act. In recent months, Europe has been engulfed in protests driven by a common crisis of affordability, deteriorating public services, and declining confidence in governments’ ability to improve living standards, especially for young people.

The protests that have swept across France, Germany, Italy, and Spain are not a single, coordinated European movement, but they reflect underlying problems that are increasingly common to a growing number of EU countries: a mounting perception that governments are unable to deliver affordable housing, functioning public services, and economic security. This widening gap between the public’s expectations and the economic and fiscal reality confronting many EU countries is only likely to grow over time. It could prompt Spanish voters to unseat Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez this weekend and usher in a right-wing government that includes the far-right party Vox. And it sets up 2027 to be a strikingly consequential year, as nine elections across the EU could completely remake the continent’s complexion.

Four of those elections, in France, Germany, Italy, and Poland, will be particularly important. In each of them, far-right populist forces could rise to the fore and turn their countries against greater EU integration, support for Ukraine, and collective European security. But dangers remain even if the far right does not win outright at the polls. A plethora of electoral outcomes could still see new governments pull away from Brussels and vitiate attempts to strengthen European autonomy and defense. Instead of a bang, the European project could die with a whimper.

The most consequential race will be in France. A series of domestic and international developments, along with a volatile national mood, have combined to make Marine Le Pen the front-runner to win the April 2027 election and become France’s first far-right president since the dissolution of the Vichy regime in 1944.

In October, riots and looting marred a series of mostly peaceful protests against high prices and deteriorating schools in a score of French cities. Those images—along with the diffuse sense of a country in paralysis—have shredded whatever remained of the domestic credibility of President Emmanuel Macron and his minority centrist coalition. Édouard Philippe, the strongest centrist contender to succeed Macron, has been tarnished by association. The first poll since the riots, conducted by IFOP for Le Figaro, showed Le Pen’s support among potential voters rising to 36 percent, her highest level to date, while support for Philippe fell three points from a month earlier to 15 percent.

Philippe and the other centrist challenger, Gabriel Attal, another Macron-era prime minister, were already losing support because of high energy prices caused by the wars in Iran and Ukraine. Both candidates remain stuck between seven percent and 15 percent in the polls, and their campaigns have generated little momentum or popular enthusiasm.

Meanwhile, an opportunistic coalition of far-left extremists and young looters from the poor multiracial suburbs of French cities who were responsible for much of the violence in October will hurt the far-left candidate Jean-Luc Mélenchon, who has been accused by the government of helping incite the unrest. He has certainly failed to condemn the disturbances. But the disorder has not dampened his allure, as support for his candidacy has risen to between 17 and 19 percent in polling, which makes him a credible contender to reach the second-round runoff against Le Pen. Unfortunately for Mélenchon, polls suggest that Le Pen would thump him in the second round, by at least 65 percent to 35 percent.

The election seems very much Le Pen’s to lose. No presidential candidate since the Fifth Republic began in 1958 has held such a commanding first-round lead in opinion polls five months before election day and lost.

A Le Pen presidency would be nothing short of an earthquake for Europe. It would deliver a death blow to the EU’s ambition of greater “strategic autonomy”: Brussels’ goal of achieving greater political independence from the United States and China, primarily via more European integration. This was a vision espoused by Macron in a speech at the Sorbonne in September 2017, in which he called for a “sovereign, united, and democratic Europe.” Le Pen is committed to unilaterally (and illegally) withholding French contributions to the EU budget; to ending French and EU aid to Ukraine; to restraining all EU spending; to blocking the enlargement of the EU to neighboring countries; and changing the French constitution to enshrine “national” primacy in jobs and welfare, which would effectively destroy the European single market. She may be denied some of these ambitions, but a hostile Paris could very easily stymie any forward movement of the EU.

Centrist governments are on the defensive beyond France, as voters across the continent conclude that they do not have the solutions to address their problems. Germany is a case in point. A weak and unpopular chancellor, Friedrich Merz, is finding it difficult to hold off the rise of the far-right, neo-Nazi Alternative for Germany party, known as the AfD, which continues to make sweeping electoral gains across the country.

Merz has to steward an economy that is struggling after decades serving as Europe’s industrial core. Although it is true that Germany’s economic outlook in 2026 and 2027 has brightened after almost flat growth in 2025, its recovery is driven exclusively by the government’s debt-financed investment programs. Key industries, including those to do with automobiles, machinery, pharmaceuticals, and chemicals, are still suffering from high energy prices, crumbling infrastructure, and a shrinking workforce.

China’s aggressive trade practices, deliberately aimed at undermining some of Germany’s core sectors, continue to put pressure on German companies. The unwinding of the international trade system also disproportionately hurts Germany’s export-driven economy. So does Europe’s fragmented and undercapitalized banking sector, which provides too little venture capital for German start-ups to scale up quickly. For these reasons, Berlin will have a hard time limiting significant job losses in its industrial sectors in 2027.

The result will be unemployment and growing electoral disgruntlement that Merz’s government will be poorly placed to address. If anything, the divides between the chancellor’s conservative camp and his coalition partner, the center-left Social Democrats, will prevent the government from delivering decisive reforms related to pensions, taxes, health care, the labor market, energy, and climate.

Five state elections in 2027, two of which are in the key states of North Rhine–Westphalia and Lower Saxony, will exacerbate this divide. Merz’s Christian Democrats will be vying for seats with the Social Democrats, keeping the two sides in a permanent competition that could prevent the coalition from governing effectively at the federal level. And that competition will likely be in vain: state election results for both parties will almost certainly be disappointing, leading to growing restiveness and speculation about Merz’s future as chancellor.

These centrifugal forces within the government will collide with the determination of both parties to stay in the coalition and avoid provoking a fresh election. This will result in a largely paralyzed government, discredited centrist politics, and a widening sense of malaise across an already unhappy and disaffected electorate.

As the center struggles, the AfD on the far right and Die Linke on the hard left will continue to gather national momentum. For the first time, the AfD will almost certainly penetrate deeply into the electorates of Germany’s western industrial strongholds in state elections next year, hollowing out the constituencies of both Christian Democrats and Social Democrats. With its sights on the 2029 federal elections, the AfD will increasingly look like a national contender rather than a protest movement rooted in eastern Germany. Die Linke, for its part, will continue to siphon off votes from left-leaning parties with its platform of economic grievance, disinterest in support for Ukraine, and the highly successful mobilization of young voters around support for Palestinians. The gains of both parties will further splinter state parliaments and make the process of government formation at that level increasingly cumbersome. The resulting state administrations will likely be unstable and doomed to performing poorly, deepening voter disillusionment with party politics and encouraging a greater willingness to experiment with radicalism.

These electoral pressures in Germany and in France make it likely that the relationship between Berlin and Paris will grow strained. A nationalist-populist president Le Pen would have little interest in sustaining the French-German “engine”—the traditional motor of European integration. Le Pen’s political priority of cutting French payments to the EU would rankle Germany, the largest contributor to the EU budget. And any government in Berlin would be unlikely to accommodate French positions, especially those of a disruptive Le Pen presidency, such as weakening the single market or abandoning Ukraine.

Without convergence between French and German policy, it will be harder for the EU’s other states to build consensus on the economic and security challenges that matter most. This prospect has encouraged European policymakers to look east, toward Poland, and ask what “new Europe” can do if “old Europe” ceases to drive the EU forward. Yet Warsaw faces its own political troubles, and it will struggle to galvanize the continent if France and Germany fall away.

Poland will stage consequential parliamentary elections next year. The vote could bring the overthrow of the current centrist coalition and the creation of a far-right coalition government involving the right-wing Law and Justice (PiS) party, the far-right Confederation Liberty and Independence, and the far-right Confederation of the Polish Crown (KKP).

A coalition formed by all three would mark a significant shift in Poland’s domestic and foreign policy. Such a government would adopt a more confrontational approach toward the EU, particularly on the issues of migration, climate policy, judicial independence, and the next seven-year EU budget. Although PiS remains broadly supportive of NATO and Ukraine, Confederation and especially the KKP have adopted a more critical stance toward Kyiv. Their participation in the government could therefore erode Poland’s support for Ukraine, including its military and financial assistance to its embattled neighbor, and lead to tougher policies regarding Ukrainian refugees, nearly one million of whom remain in Poland after fleeing Russia’s full-scale invasion in 2022, according to Eurostat.

Significant ideological differences among the three parties, particularly over economic policy, would probably make this far-right coalition unstable. PiS’s preference for social spending and state intervention sits uneasily alongside Confederation’s economically liberal agenda. Moreover, the leader of PiS, Jaroslaw Kaczynski, has repeatedly ruled out cooperation with KKP’s leader, Grzegorz Braun, describing him as “very dangerous” and his party as “completely unserious.” A far-right government could be quite short-lived, if it emerges at all.

An alternative but equally problematic scenario would involve a hung parliament in which neither Prime Minister Donald Tusk’s Civic Coalition (KO) nor PiS could assemble a governing majority, resulting in a weak minority government or even fresh elections. Fragmentation among smaller parties and disagreements over potential coalition partners could prolong negotiations, particularly if PiS refuses to cooperate with KKP while Tusk’s coalition lacks sufficient support from centrist and left-wing parties. Even if a minority government were formed, its dependence on opposition parties for legislative support could severely constrain policymaking, leaving Poland unable to address its mounting security challenges or play any leadership role in Brussels.

Italy, meanwhile, presents a different risk. Unlike France, Germany, or Poland, it currently has a relatively stable government led by a prime minister, Giorgi Meloni, who has reconciled her far-right nationalist origins with a broadly pragmatic approach to European and foreign affairs. Indeed, Meloni has become something of a poster child of far-right moderation, held up as evidence that ultranationalists can become responsible European leaders. But that equilibrium could be upset by the emergence of a new political force even further to Meloni’s right.

Meloni remains the favorite to win next year’s election. But she is threatened by Roberto Vannacci, a former paratrooper and veteran of the Iraq and Afghanistan wars who has launched a new party, National Future (FN), that is peeling away right-wing voters from Meloni with a nationalist, populist agenda that is skeptical of NATO and support for Ukraine. His goal is to court Italians aggrieved by Meloni’s compromises with Brussels.

The worst-case scenario would be a right-wing majority in which FN holds the balance of power and chooses to use it. That outcome would require the party to outperform expectations, approaching or exceeding ten percent of the vote—FN is currently polling at 6.5 percent—while Meloni’s Brothers of Italy party slips to around 20 percent or below. The coalition would still govern, but its center of gravity would shift decisively to the right, and Meloni would no longer have a firm grip at the helm.

Vannacci would likely not moderate his positions once in government, as Meloni did. Instead, he would maximize his leverage, up to and including threatening to pull out of the coalition and trigger snap elections, to extract concessions regarding Italy’s relations to Europe. His strategy would be deliberately confrontational: squeezing Meloni between the demands of his right-wing base and the constraints imposed by the EU, NATO, and the bond markets. The aim would be less to shape policy than to build his own visibility and lure supporters away from Meloni’s Brothers of Italy. Faced with a direct challenge on her right flank, Meloni would be forced to prioritize her own political survival, pulling the government toward more unorthodox positions.

The effects would be most acute in foreign policy, as Italy moved from the consensual position it has adopted on Ukraine to more active opposition, a major challenge at a time when EU decision-making on Ukraine, defense, and the EU budget depends heavily on unanimity among the EU’s largest members. Italy could transform from a pragmatic, generally constructive player in Brussels into a more disruptive force, blocking or threatening to veto decisions on bilateral and EU-wide support for Ukraine. Alongside a far-right Paris, Rome would also block Ukraine’s path to EU accession. On EU financial matters, Italy would straddle Germany and France, siding with Berlin in opposing any increase in the bloc’s next budget and with Paris in resisting cuts to agriculture and funding for the EU’s poorest regions, narrowing the space for a deal on the EU’s next seven-year budget and certainly curtailing its geopolitical ambitions. Under pressure from Vannacci, Meloni could also be forced to abandon NATO’s target for member states to spend five percent of GDP on their militaries and even forgo her planned incremental increases in military spending.

The government would push hard for more flexibility on EU fiscal targets and, in a more extreme scenario, breach the rules outright, reviving bond market volatility. On migration, Italy could refuse to take back asylum seekers from other member states under the EU’s Dublin rules, undermining implementation of the bloc’s migration pact.

In considering these elections together, it becomes clear that the danger facing the EU is not that the union will suddenly collapse but that the political consensus that underpins European integration could begin to unravel in its key member states. The risks are not all equal. A Le Pen presidency is the most likely and most consequential for the EU, while political paralysis in Germany also looks increasingly difficult to avoid. Although Poland’s elections could produce a moderate center-right government, a far-right government or a period of protracted instability now looks increasingly plausible. Italy’s trajectory is perhaps the least threatening, but a government dependent on Vannacci would create another impediment to broader European cooperation.

Nor do all four races need to deliver the worst-case scenarios for the consequences in the EU to be far-reaching. A Le Pen victory in France combined with paralysis in Germany would itself throw up major problems for the EU, including a possible bond market crisis in France that could infect the broader eurozone. If Poland and Italy also moved in that direction, the EU could find itself without a functioning coalition of major states willing and able to address the growing security and economic challenges the continent is facing.

The biggest victim of these looming changes is likely Ukraine and, by extension, Europe’s own security. The burden of supporting Ukraine financially would fall on a smaller group of member states in northwestern Europe, but they by themselves would not be able to fully compensate for the loss of support from the likes of France and Germany.

The EU has survived previous internal and external crises because its major powers ultimately judged that their interests were better served by collaboration and integration than by going their own way. The danger in 2027 is that this election supercycle will deliver outcomes that mean this assumption no longer holds. Even if the EU perseveres through the upheaval ahead, it could lose something much more profound: the underlying political consensus that has allowed the bloc to function since its creation, seven decades ago. That would not be the formal end of the European project. But it could mark the beginning of its irreversible decline.

#5854. From turkey shortages to helicopter deliveries: The extraordinary history of Thanksgiving during war

House lawmakers on both sides of the aisle spoke to Fox News Digital about what they are most thankful for as they get ready to celebrate Thanksgiving with their families.

In November 1966, 12 American soldiers stationed at an isolated outpost in a Vietnamese war zone were about as far from a traditional Thanksgiving at home as they could get.

Their only means of resupply was by air. But the U.S. military was determined to get them Thanksgiving dinner anyway.

A mess sergeant at Bien Hoa prepared turkey and all the trimmings, packed the meal into insulated containers and sent it by helicopter into War Zone D, where three officers and nine enlisted men of a 5th Special Forces detachment sat down to Thanksgiving dinner, according to Defense Department records preserved by the National Archives.

The delivery was one piece of a massive logistical effort the U.S. military has undertaken during wars spanning generations to get traditional Thanksgiving meals to troops serving in combat zones.

Soldiers are pictured eating Thanksgiving dinner during World War I, in 1918. (National Archives)

From civilians sending tens of thousands of turkeys to Union troops during the Civil War to helicopters carrying hot meals to soldiers in Korea, the holiday has been observed through some of the nation's most consequential conflicts.

Thanksgiving became a national tradition in the midst of war. President Abraham Lincoln issued his 1863 Thanksgiving proclamation as the Civil War entered its third year, calling on Americans to observe the final Thursday of November as a day of "Thanksgiving and Praise."

By the following year, Americans on the home front were mounting a massive effort to get Thanksgiving dinner to Union troops.

Former War Department official Charles A. Dana later recalled the "great turkey movement" of November 1864. In New York alone, organizers reported having 20,000 turkeys ready to send to the front, according to Dana's account. A Philadelphia committee sought transportation for another 4,000 destined for troops serving under Gen. Philip Sheridan.

U.S. service members stationed at an airbase in Norfolk, England, select turkeys for their Thanksgiving dinners at a local farm on Nov. 6, 1943. (National Archives)

By World War II, the effort to put turkey on troops' Thanksgiving tables had grown so large that it affected whether Americans at home could find one for their own.

In August 1943, the War Food Administration restricted most civilian turkey sales as the government sought millions of pounds for the armed forces. An initial goal of at least 10 million pounds for troops overseas was expanded to include military installations at home, and the government ultimately obtained roughly 30 million pounds before the order was lifted in October, according to wartime government records.

A year later, delivering Thanksgiving dinner near the front could carry its own risks.

102-YEAR-OLD NAVY VETERAN STILL CRAVES HOT WINGS AFTER 102 COMBAT MISSIONS IN KOREA: 'I MIGHT MAKE IT TO 115'

Sailors Leo F. DesJarlait, left, Eulice E. Wheeler, center, and G.J. Lindner eat turkey and trimmings at an advanced U.S. Navy base in the Aleutian Islands on Nov. 24, 1944. Lindner gives a "4.0" sign, meaning "perfect," in response to the Thanksgiving meal. (National Archives)

In Germany's Hürtgen Forest, Lt. Paul Boesch of the 121st Infantry Regiment later recalled being told that hot turkey dinners had arrived for his men amid some of the European campaign's most grueling fighting. Boesch protested that sending soldiers to collect the food would expose them to German artillery, but the order to distribute the meal stood.

His fears proved well-founded. As troops gathered to retrieve the food, German artillery struck the area, killing and wounding several men, according to Boesch's account.

Six years later, American troops were again eating Thanksgiving dinner near the front, this time in the bitter cold of North Korea.

On Thanksgiving Day 1950, Marines around the Chosin Reservoir were served turkey and cranberry sauce, candied sweet potatoes, fruitcake and mince pie. In the subzero temperatures, the food quickly began to freeze.

US NAVY RELEASES PHOTOS OF 'FRESH MEALS,' PUSHES BACK ON REPORTS OF FOOD SHORTAGES ON MIDDLE EAST WARSHIPS

The dinner came as U.S. commanders remained optimistic that the war could soon be over. The following day, Gen. Douglas MacArthur launched an offensive that became known as the "Home-by-Christmas" campaign.

Instead, Chinese forces entered the fighting in massive numbers. Within days of Thanksgiving, Marines around Chosin were surrounded and battling through one of the most brutal engagements of the Korean War.

Pfc. John A. Arnett, left, receives a turkey leg from Cpl. Fred Burks during Thanksgiving dinner in Korea on Nov. 22, 1951. The chow line was set up on the back side of a hill roughly 200 yards from the main line of resistance. (National Archives)

More than half a century later, another generation of American troops was spending Thanksgiving at war in Iraq and Afghanistan.

In 2003, President George W. Bush secretly flew into Baghdad to surprise roughly 600 troops gathered for Thanksgiving dinner at Baghdad International Airport. Air Force One approached with its cabin shades drawn and lights dimmed amid tight security surrounding the trip.

"I was just looking for a warm meal somewhere," Bush joked before addressing the troops and helping serve Thanksgiving dinner.

In Afghanistan, getting that dinner to troops stationed far from major bases could require a different kind of operation.

President George H.W. Bush eats Thanksgiving dinner on the hood of a Humvee while visiting U.S. troops in Saudi Arabia during Operation Desert Shield on Nov. 22, 1990. (National Archives)

On Thanksgiving 2007, helicopters carried turkeys and traditional fixings from Bagram Airfield to soldiers at remote firebases in the Tagab Valley, according to a contemporaneous U.S. military account.

It was the first hot meal some of the soldiers had eaten in months. Because the positions still had to be defended, troops rotated between Thanksgiving dinner and standing watch.

"The white meat was magnificent," Pfc. Matt Lorimer of the 82nd Airborne Division said.

President George W. Bush traveled to Iraq in 2003 to meet with troops without the public's knowledge. (Tim Sloan/AFP/Getty Images)

At another remote Afghan outpost two years later, troops spent a month fattening five scrawny turkeys purchased locally for Thanksgiving. But five birds were not enough to feed roughly 150 soldiers.

Additional turkey was flown from the United States to Bagram Airfield and then transported to the outpost. Even that was not the end of the journey.

A handful of soldiers were manning an observation post on a mountain spur roughly 6,900 feet above sea level. Their Thanksgiving dinner was packed up and flown by helicopter up the mountain to them.

#5853. Big Tech Has Undermined Democracy. Here’s How to Reverse the Trend.

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Argument: Big Tech Has Undermined Democracy. Here’s How to Reverse the Trend.

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

It is now common to lament a crisis in the system of democracy. Confidence in public institutions has declined, experts are dismissed, and politics is becoming a contest over not only values and interests but reality itself.

These trends have come alongside a corresponding rise in attempts to explain this phenomenon, ranging from feckless leadership and rising inequality to globalization and economic shocks. While those are all factors, a more compelling explanation could lie in how the digital revolution has undermined trust in facts, governments, and democracy itself.

It is now common to lament a crisis in the system of democracy. Confidence in public institutions has declined, experts are dismissed, and politics is becoming a contest over not only values and interests but reality itself.

These trends have come alongside a corresponding rise in attempts to explain this phenomenon, ranging from feckless leadership and rising inequality to globalization and economic shocks. While those are all factors, a more compelling explanation could lie in how the digital revolution has undermined trust in facts, governments, and democracy itself.

To be clear, falsehoods and disinformation are not new. Manipulating information has long been a tool of statecraft. The ancient Indian political treatise, the Arthashastra, advised rulers to spread rumors to control public opinion. But while modern media has vastly expanded the scale, scope, and reach of information, it has also turbocharged the dissemination of propaganda and misinformation.

This degradation of informational systems is precipitating a crisis of legitimacy in the core institutions of democracies. Meanwhile, there is a growing mismatch between inherited democratic institutions and our new social realities. In some countries, the central problem is the decay of once-strong mass intermediaries such as political parties, unions, a free press, and civic organizations. In others, mass intermediaries never achieved the authority imagined in the classic democratic model. In others, the state itself remains too uneven or predatory to serve as a reliable object of trust. But in all cases, democracies face a similar problem: how to make collective self-government credible when citizens no longer accept inherited intermediaries as authoritative or trustworthy.

What is the solution? There is no easy answer, but a path to restoring faith in democracy can only begin by making the democratic process more observable, relational, and capable. And this process must take place first at the local level, building from the bottom up—just as democracy was designed to do.

Audience members hold up their phones during fashion week in Sao Paulo on Oct. 28, 2018. Mauricio Santana/Getty Images

Trust—among citizens and between citizens and their governments—is the bedrock of democracy. Trust represents a pact between the trustor—the party extending the trust—and the trustee, or the party being trusted. Such a transaction requires a degree of vulnerability on the part of the trustor. Trust is a psychological condition that allows the trustor to set aside a purely rational calculus and make a leap of faith toward positive expectations about the trustee.

We can distinguish between two types of trust: cognition-based and affect-based. The former is an evidence-driven process by which individuals use updated information to judge the reliability, character, and ability of the trustee. Affect-based trust, in contrast, underpins social relationships and is based on emotions of care and concern for others.

In politics, cognitive trust is based on calculated assessments of the competence of politicians and governments based on tangible metrics such as economic growth, inflation, and crime. To feel this form of trust, voters need to have access to information they find reliable and the bandwidth to process it. This is decreasingly the case.

What is left is affective trust. This is driven by emotional bonds and shared identities and values. Affective judgments lead citizens to base their voting decisions on the person rather than the policies, on feelings rather than facts. This matters because new media—especially social media—is particularly adept at amplifying emotionally charged discourse. Such narratives are stickier than verifiable facts, and their participatory nature converts audiences from passive consumers to active co-authors churning out their own alternative narratives. These dynamics become even more febrile in an era of uncertainty. The result is emotionally driven discourse increasingly shaping public opinion, with facts becoming collateral damage.

A conversation with Nobel laureate Maria Ressa.

The biggest risks of AI and other advanced technologies have less to do with their growing power than the men behind them.

Silicon Valley is becoming an instrument of U.S. coercion, and that’s a danger to each and every country.

Voters repose both cognition-based and affect-based trust in politicians and the state. But to the extent the information environment deteriorates—what Steve Bannon, U.S. President Donald Trump’s former advisor, once referred to as “flooding the zone”—the attention economy constricts cognitive bandwidth. The result, as Herbert Simon famously noted, is a “poverty of attention.” The arc of trust then bends toward affective trust. Emotional attachments to personalities acquire greater salience. This may explain, at least partly, the current wave of populism.

In 2023, 44 percent of citizens surveyed by the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development reported no or low trust in their governments. In 2025, in the United States, trust in government fell to near its lowest recorded level, with just 17 percent of Americans saying they trusted the federal government to do what is right.

Democracy depends on trust at scale. A resident of New Jersey will rarely meet an Alaskan. In the modern era, their sense of collective fate is bound together by shared sources of authority, such as official documents, and means of disseminating commonly trusted information, such as news broadcasts. If achieving broad impersonal trust was one of the unsung accomplishments of the modern state, this was made possible by mass institutions such as unions, parties, and a reliable media ecosystem.

Both mass institutions and impersonal trust are deteriorating. Given the structure of the current information environment, these are unlikely to be revived. As mass trust has fragmented, particularized trust—in personalities, partisan leaders, or online communities—seems to be on the rise. There is no source of certification that is accepted at scale.

Misinformation is hard to defeat with truth alone; it requires truth to be tethered to a trusted authority transmitting the message. As trust has fragmented, attacking misinformation has become more complicated, requiring more than mere correction. When societies no longer trust a single common referee, it is hard to achieve broad acceptance of the contest they adjudicate.

A voter exits a booth at a polling station in Lancaster, New Hampshire, on Nov. 5, 2024.Joseph Prezioso/AFP via Getty Images

Where do we go from here? We believe that rebuilding citizens’ trust in their governments will increasingly require the lived experience of national polities to occur at smaller scales where citizens can see public purpose enacted. Such public policy would be made not only in legislatures and ministries but also in the everyday discretion of frontline workers.

This is one reason that street-level bureaucracy matters so much. Citizens rarely encounter a national government. Instead, their primary encounters are with the local state—a teacher, a social worker, a police officer, a sanitation worker, a building inspector, a nurse, a benefits caseworker, a public meeting, a pothole crew, a school board, or a public transport employee. These encounters represent democracy’s front line. They are the key to a more capable state, which in turn boosts the state’s legitimacy and builds trust.

Smaller administrative units are more conducive for building trust because they can collectively draw on tacit knowledge and shared networks, repeat interactions, and direct observation. Engagement at this local level with the broader state can build relational state capacity—reciprocal relationships where both parties feel seen and understood by the other. Local units are primary sites of trust formation, handling decisions that depend heavily on contextual knowledge and observable accountability. Regional or national government can support the local, but in this model, trust ultimately flows up from the local rather than down from a pinnacle source, be that a Walter Cronkite, the United Nations, or a national leader.

Three principles are fundamental to creating this new reality. First, democracy must become more observable. Currently, citizens are asked to trust many things they cannot personally experience, such as central bank decisions, vaccine approvals, or election integrity. Some of this is unavoidable. A meeting to set a central bank’s interest rate will inevitably be an elite club. But many of these things don’t need to be opaque. Election infrastructure is needs greater transparency, incorporating community at the local level; many more opportunities for sustained citizen interaction, personal observation, and collective public judgment can and should be found. Citizens must be able to connect what they observe to public purpose. More quotidian, regular interaction with the state—not more online portals or structured public hearings—will help democracies build trust. Public work should be organized so that its progress, purpose, and challenges are intelligible to everyday citizens.

Second, democracy must become more relational. Trust will not emerge from public information messaging, but rather from repeated interactions where people can update their beliefs about one another. Mutual respect is key to interactions that build trust and expand the definition of community. Citizens do not need more exposure to opposing views; they need structured settings in which disagreement is part of a collective effort to constructively govern. Schools, juries, unions, and civic organizations show us the potential of these types of forums. Citizens must have the opportunity to assess whether officials listen, keep commitments, and exercise judgment with care. Officials, in turn, need to gain knowledge about citizens that administrative categories alone cannot supply. Building local trust requires rethinking how we create space for relationship building and empowerment of front-line workers. It also requires institutions that provide attentive treatment even to those who arrive without influence or connections.

Third, democracy must become more capable. The administrative state is the front door of democracy; it cannot be an afterthought. Simply put, the state must be able to deliver on its promises. Capability is not merely technical prowess, however. Complex collective problems often require multiple centers of decision-making, local knowledge, monitoring, and rule adaptation, as well as coproduction between the state and society. Front-line workers need not only have skills but also authority, organizational support, and flexibility for the state to become more capable.

Focusing on local communities can have a surprising international effect. By Gordon Brown

It is tempting to respond to malfeasance and unequal treatment with layers of compliance such as rules, audits, targets, and procedural safeguards. Some are necessary. But when we set things up so bad that actions can never happen again, as contrite managers often say, we consign ourselves to a suboptimal, constrained state. Too much regulation makes public servants less able to solve problems or act in ways that citizens recognize as responsive. A state that exercises judgment capriciously and arbitrarily will not earn citizens’ trust, but neither will a state that cannot exercise judgment at all.

These principles are meant to complement rather than substitute for national and transnational units of government. The national unit cannot be a mere composite of local experience. States will need to adapt to the changing architecture of trust, which will require rethinking accountability. A focus on thin metrics will need to be replaced with a broader collective justification. That channel of accountability should, whenever possible, run from those engaging with citizens to those citizens themselves, rather than up the hierarchy to supervisors in a distant office. National institutions can rebuild trust through more reliable local encounters that their resources and coordination make possible.

However, these principles will have to confront the elephant in the room—technology. In recent years, new technologies, first digital and now artificial intelligence, have been fanning the fires of mistrust. National governments will especially need to play an active role in facilitating robust safety standards for new technologies. It will be exceedingly difficult to rebuild trust without first strengthening faith in these technologies themselves. Historically, technology standards have been built by professionals within the sector—not through government regulation—recognizing that guardrails are fundamental to building trust and thereby the long-term prospects of the technology. After the internet was established, the refusal of the tech giants to build strong universal safety standards sowed the very seeds of mistrust that are now coming back to haunt them. If this occurs with artificial intelligence, a much more consequential technology, the fallout will be even more dire.

Youth gather at a community center in downtown Rio de Janeiro in October 2007. Tom Stoddart/Getty Images

Democracy first flourished in settings where citizens could assemble face to face. Mass institutions and new technologies allowed democracy to scale. One consequence was that the primacy of the local as the locus of trust, and ultimately where democracy thrived, withered. More recently, the deluge of information has also vastly increased the speed and scope of misinformation. But going forward, information will be increasingly produced—and consumed—by machines. The ructions from this shift will profoundly change the informational landscape and, with it, the cognitive foundations of democracy.

Rebuilding and nourishing the local roots of democracy should become an urgent imperative for societies, not simply in a geographically proximate sense, but incorporating any forum where members come to accept and trust one another. For governments, the lesson is not to control or dictate to these communities, but to scaffold and connect them. This, in turn, requires making the state more capable, more relational, and more observable. Governments will also have to coordinate to build stronger safety standards for the technologies that are enmeshed in the burgeoning labyrinths of mistrust.

Democracy will only be rebuilt if the corrosiveness of mistrust is reversed. We must accept what we cannot change—that the structure of information has drastically changed, and there will be no innovation that yields a rebirth of mass, impersonal democracy. The future of democratic trust depends not on our screens but on our neighborhoods. If we can no longer trust from the top down, then we must rebuild it from the bottom up.

Dan Honig is an associate professor at Georgetown University’s McCourt School of Public Policy and University College London’s department of political science.

Devesh Kapur is the Starr Foundation professor of South Asian studies at the School of Advanced International Studies at Johns Hopkins University.

#5851. The U.S.-China Endgame on AI

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MATT SHEEHAN is a Senior Fellow at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace and Co-Director of its China AI Initiative.

SCOTT SINGER is a Fellow at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace and Co-Director of its China AI Initiative.

Time may be running out for the United States and China to avert an artificial intelligence crisis. Many of the researchers and engineers building the world’s most powerful frontier AI models believe they may be only a year or two away from achieving recursive self-improvement—the ability of AI systems to build smarter versions of themselves. Once that threshold is crossed, models could quickly develop beyond human control. Although the potential timeline and consequences of recursive self-improvement are hotly debated, any U.S. strategy on AI must take seriously the possibility that its arrival could be imminent.

But in Washington, concerns about the dangers of a near-term “intelligence explosion” are colliding with the geopolitical reality that the United States is locked in a fierce AI race with China. Many policymakers oppose more regulation because they fear it will cause the United States to fall behind. Indeed, many frontier labs continue accelerating the automation of AI development, despite warnings by their own employees that slowing down the pace of progress might be necessary to make the technology safe.

The United States urgently needs a strategy to navigate this dilemma. Yet national security experts, AI researchers, and elected officials fundamentally disagree about what Washington should seek to achieve and how to manage competition with Beijing.

Thus far, three competing visions have emerged. The first seeks a full U.S. victory, or what is often called decisive strategic advantage, over China. Proponents of this perspective believe that the U.S.-Chinese AI race has a finish line: the achievement of artificial superintelligence, or AI with capabilities that far exceed those of humans across nearly all domains. In this worldview, safety is best achieved through dominance, and the United States needs to win the AI race to be able to impose necessary safeguards around the world. A second vision, which we call “AI safety in parallel,” recognizes the need for risk mitigation but proposes that much of this can happen independently on each side. Thus, both China and the United States should pursue their own safety measures—and share best practices with each other—while they continue to compete to advance the technology. A third view argues that AI safety can only be assured by institutionalized cooperation between the United States and China. In this vision, the two countries need a binding international treaty to constrain dangerous AI development.

Although each perspective has merit, none provides the basis of a workable strategy on its own. A single-minded effort to gain a decisive advantage over China could turbocharge the race toward superintelligence before either country has developed sufficient safeguards, risking an AI-driven catastrophe or even great-power war. In turn, leaving each country to regulate its own AI industry amid ongoing competition may cause both sides to underinvest in safety. And an international treaty may be infeasible in the near term because of deep distrust between Washington and Beijing and the technical challenges of verifying an agreement.

The United States should seek to extend its technological lead over China, but it should not blindly pursue AI dominance at the expense of safety. Instead, it should enforce strict controls on chip exports to China while aggressively regulating frontier AI development at home. As Washington and Beijing work in parallel to implement their own safety measures, they can engage in dialogue to reduce shared risks without binding reciprocal commitments. Such efforts, along with investment in technology to verify compliance with safety rules, can set the foundation for a future international agreement. It is a complex balancing act, but it may be the only way to walk the narrow path needed to make it through the AI revolution safely.

Many U.S. national security strategists dream of harnessing superintelligent AI to secure an insurmountable advantage over adversaries—and fear the consequences if the United States doesn’t. In his highly influential 2024 essay “Situational Awareness,” the former AI researcher Leopold Aschenbrenner argued that superintelligence could compress a century of military progress into less than a decade, meaning that, as he argued, “Those who have it will wield complete dominance over those who don’t.” Proponents of this view speculate that superintelligence could allow a country to create impenetrable missile defense systems or infiltrate an opponent’s cyber defenses so thoroughly that it could shut down all Internet-connected electronics. Such a first-mover advantage could even make it possible to halt an opponent’s AI progress, permanently entrenching an unbeatable advantage. This vision has resonated in parts of Washington and Silicon Valley alike: in September, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent warned, “There is no day after tomorrow if China wins at this. . . . If they were to pull ahead of us on AI, then nothing else matters.”

If there were a clear way for the United States to build provably safe superintelligence, it would be sensible to accelerate AI development. In practice, however, the path to superintelligence appears increasingly treacherous. For one thing, developers are already struggling to control their AI systems, as a rash of safety incidents this summer demonstrated. Most notable was the OpenAI–Hugging Face incident, in which hundreds of AI agents escaped contained testing environments and colluded to hack an external website. Leaders of major AI labs in the United States have since called for a coordinated slowdown in development. A technological edge offers little strategic benefit to either the United States or China if it creates catastrophic risks for everyone.

Advocates of decisive advantage argue that the United States needs to establish an insurmountable lead over China in order to resolve safety challenges on Washington’s terms. Only if U.S. models are far ahead of Chinese ones—two years rather than two months, in Aschenbrenner’s telling—will the United States have enough time to slow down AI development and focus on safety. In this view, the imminent attainment of superintelligence will force China to accept a U.S.-led AI order, including its safety norms.

The United States has tools at its disposal to try to extend its lead in AI. Earlier this month, Washington restricted Beijing’s access to advanced chips in data centers overseas, jeopardizing a meaningful source of computing power for Chinese AI companies. The United States could further constrain China’s ability to develop AI by ramping up export controls on the semiconductor manufacturing equipment that China needs to produce its own chips. Policymakers in Washington could also work with American AI companies to prevent China’s labs from engaging in industrial-scale distillation, the process by which they shortcut development by training their models on existing American AI.

But these measures cannot reverse China’s AI development. Despite U.S. restrictions, there are already large numbers of leading-edge Nvidia chips inside China, and Chinese technology giant Huawei continues to produce weaker but still usable chips domestically. If China consolidates its existing computing resources, it can continue to advance the capabilities of its leading models. And if the push toward recursive self-improvement continues apace, the United States will not have time to build a commanding lead before safety issues come to a head.

The difficulty of securing AI models also makes the pursuit of decisive advantage dangerous. Although it takes enormous quantities of computing power to train an AI model, in its finished form it is a digital file small enough to fit on an external hard drive. American AI labs and the national security community are taking steps to protect these files. But leakage is always a risk, and it will only grow as the technology becomes more powerful. If the United States’ goal is to achieve total hegemony through a superintelligent AI model—one that can devastate all of a geopolitical rival’s defenses—then Washington must be absolutely sure that China will never be able to access, steal, or sabotage that model.

Moreover, even if the United States were able to maintain a global lead in AI capabilities, it would not necessarily translate into a decisive military advantage. So long as states have nuclear weapons and can credibly threaten to use them, no amount of AI power can coerce them into submission. As Sam Winter-Levy and Nikita Lalwani explained in Foreign Affairs, in 2025, “Even the most sophisticated AI-powered targeting and sensor systems may struggle to locate a mobile nuclear launcher hidden under a bridge, isolate the signatures of a nuclear-armed submarine from the background noise of the ocean, and orchestrate the simultaneous destruction of hundreds of targets on land, air, and sea—with zero room for error.” The president would need to be so confident that the technology would wipe out an opponent’s entire arsenal that he or she would risk a devastating nuclear strike on U.S. soil, a possibility that no leader is likely willing to countenance.

The pursuit of decisive strategic advantage could even provoke the very conflict it seeks to prevent. If achieving permanent dominance were technically possible, China would have strong incentives to prevent the United States from achieving such an advantage. It could launch preemptive strikes targeting American data centers or semiconductor manufacturing hubs in Taiwan. In a cruel twist of fate, pursuit of a decisive advantage to secure lasting American-led peace could be a potential cause of catastrophic devastation.

The United States has sound reasons to seek an AI advantage over China. But trying to maintain a technological lead is not the same as pursuing permanent dominance at any cost. Washington cannot assume that if it accelerates AI development now, it will have enough time to resolve core safety issues later. If the United States and China remain at or near the AI frontier, they will need to find a way to manage these risks, either on their own terms or with each other.

In a world in which neither the United States nor China has figured out how to build superintelligence safely, each will need to take steps to rein in the technology. As rogue agents and intentional misuse of powerful systems threaten public safety, both governments will increasingly face enormous pressure to mitigate those risks, regardless of what their adversary does.

On its own, China has already implemented the world’s most extensive AI regulations. Beijing requires Chinese AI companies to undergo extensive testing to show that their models are unlikely to violate the country’s censorship restrictions, and it mandates safeguards around products such as AI companions that could cause psychological harm. This does not mean that China is addressing potential catastrophic risks: safety practices among Chinese AI labs lag significantly behind those of their American peers. But the regulatory framework does show that Beijing is willing to sacrifice some technological development and impose a substantial compliance burden on its companies.

Concerns among Chinese leaders about the potential loss of human control over AI have risen significantly in the past year, even reaching the pages of the Chinese Communist Party’s flagship theory journal this month. Party leaders want Chinese models to rival those in the United States. But given the CCP’s penchant for control, if China were to catch up to the United States it is likely that Beijing would be cautious about further accelerating development. The notion that Chinese leaders would see the potential dangers of superintelligence and encourage AI labs to plow full speed ahead runs counter to the regime’s core desire for control and stability.

In the United States, Congress has not legislated frontier AI development. The White House, for its part, has imposed opaque demands on companies, with the government vetting models for safety risks. The most systematic efforts at regulation are taking place at the state level. In 2025, California passed an AI safety law that requires frontier AI companies to explain how they mitigate catastrophic risks and report critical safety incidents. A year later, the state’s leaders recommended that the government be able to cap recursive self-improvement and require companies to show that they can shut down dangerous models. There is an expanding cohort in Congress voicing support for writing similar measures into federal law.

Although both the United States and China may be unilaterally motivated to pursue safety measures, their choices are inevitably connected. Each has a strong interest in the safety of the other’s systems because a model trained in Shanghai could wreak havoc in Silicon Valley, or vice versa. The two superpowers can pursue domestic regulation in parallel and use their own efforts as grounds for engagement: they can exchange best practices for mitigating AI risk and can share information on emerging safety incidents. These efforts would help demystify intentions and prevent dangerous misunderstandings from spiraling out of control. Measures implemented by one side also serve as a signal that it is genuinely committed to AI safety, which can encourage similar action by the other.

Despite its mixed messages on domestic safety regulation, the White House has laid the foundations to pursue safety in parallel with Beijing. In September, the two sides agreed to kickstart an AI safety dialogue and share information on incidents. And earlier this month, a few days before U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping met in Shenzhen, China, Bessent announced that the United States and China plan to use existing AI talks for exchanging best practices on testing and evaluation. Although these efforts remain tentative and fragile, they are an important starting point for collaboration.

An advantage of pursuing AI safety efforts in parallel is that it can be done quickly and efficiently without the ongoing, intensive, and likely drawn out coordination that an international treaty would require. But unilateral action from each side may not produce strong enough guardrails to prevent the biggest risks. Many leaders in Washington fear that any domestic slowdown in AI development will give China a chance to catch up with or even surpass the United States, and some of their counterparts in Beijing see a slowdown or pause by China as a way to lock in a U.S. lead indefinitely. As long as both sides remain more concerned about AI competition than about safety, unilateral risk mitigation measures are likely to remain inadequate.

AI has created a classic security dilemma: each side sees accelerating development as rational from a national security perspective, even though their combined actions could cause global harm. An international treaty offers one possible solution. A binding agreement would allow both the United States and China to verify compliance with a set of rules to manage, and perhaps even pause, AI development.

One model such a treaty could follow, called AI 2040, comes from researchers at the AI Futures Project, an AI forecasting nonprofit. They propose to temporarily pause the training of newer and more advanced AI models until humans can confidently mitigate the risks such models bring. The United States and China could use all of the computing power they have to run existing models—but they would agree to not use any of it to train new, more powerful models until safety concerns are fully addressed. Enforcement of the plan would rely on mutual monitoring of how computing power in each country is being used, confirming that countries and labs are running current models rather than training new ones.

If executed well, such a treaty offers an attractive balance: it would harness today’s already extraordinary AI systems to promote economic growth and encourage scientific discovery while holding off building newer systems that developers don’t yet know how to control. But negotiating such a treaty faces enormous political, geopolitical, and technical hurdles. In the United States, despite calls by AI executives and members of Congress for more international coordination, the Trump administration has generally resisted proactive efforts. And in China, although Beijing has begun to pay more attention to frontier risks, it hasn’t taken concrete action to reduce them, suggesting that it is a long way from being ready for an ambitious international solution.

Moreover, the geopolitical challenges to designing such a treaty are formidable. If both countries were to pause development where it currently stands, the United States would maintain its edge in model capabilities and China could feel that it would be locking itself into a subordinate position. Or, if the countries were to set a common redline past which models would not be permitted to develop, it would allow China to catch up and force the United States to forfeit its advantage. Each option presents one side with a bitter pill to swallow.

Even if the two sides were to reach a treaty, building the technology to verify compliance is more difficult than many observers assume. During the Cold War, the United States faced a similar verification challenge in its efforts to set up a global nonproliferation regime. AI nonproliferation will be much harder, as Christopher David LaRoche and Ankit Panda have argued in Foreign Affairs, because the inputs to AI development are harder to trace. Furthermore, any international agreement is an attempt to hit a moving target because the technology can develop faster than humans can regulate it. New forms of collaboration among AI agents could allow systems to evolve on their own even if countries agree to stop training new or larger models. By the time the ink dries on a safety agreement, it could already be headed toward obsolescence.

Still, in the long term, a verifiable and binding treaty is likely the best way to solve coordination problems between the two countries. Although a governmental push for a bilateral treaty is likely premature without further efforts to establish cooperation, that could change quickly as safety fears proliferate. Researchers are already building prototypes of technology to verify whether a data center is being used to run existing models or train new ones, similar to what is envisioned in the AI 2040 plan. Washington and Silicon Valley should greatly accelerate investment in verification technology, and how to ensure compliance should be a central focus of negotiations between the United States and China in AI. But an international agreement cannot be the only option. Safety dangers might arrive before verification technology is ready for the real world.

The United States needs an affirmative vision for how to pursue AI development and safety. The best way to get there is by sequencing and coordinating elements from each of the three perspectives. Washington should start by strongly enforcing existing chip export controls and cracking down on distillation to extend the American AI lead over China.

But as U.S. labs forge ahead, Washington must be clear about the growing risks of a technology that it does not understand and may not be able to control. It must take aggressive action to regulate new frontier models. Policymakers can require AI labs to grant continuous access to their models to independent safety evaluators, and they can mandate that all developers produce a concrete plan for how their models can be shut down in the event of an emergency. U.S. companies will also need to take initiative themselves: they will need to reallocate far more of their financial and computational resources toward improving the safety of their AI systems.

With these steps underway, the White House should use the next six months to build on the foundation of the U.S.-Chinese AI dialogue announced in September. It can do this by creating bilateral technical working groups to discuss emerging risks, such as loss of human control over models, and building channels to communicate about AI safety incidents. Repeated engagement between Washington and Beijing on concrete safety issues can reveal how each side understands the technological risks and prospects for intervention, both of which are critical to assessing the other side’s appetite for mutually binding steps. Policymakers should simultaneously scale up investments in verification technology necessary for a workable long-term treaty. The prospects of a treaty are currently slim, but ongoing cooperation and greater investment in verification technology could make an agreement possible in the future.

U.S.-Chinese relations currently structure the development of frontier AI, but they do not predetermine its path. Pursuing superintelligence is a choice. These technologies are built by and for humans, and governments—responding to their citizens—should shape how AI is used. As the day fast approaches when humans may no longer be able to fully control the most advanced models, what Washington needs is a combination of political courage, technical ingenuity, and diplomatic creativity to seize the moment so that it can work with, and in parallel to, China to avoid potential catastrophe.

#5850. Mamdani handed out $95K in staff raises the same day he warned of $12B budget deficit: report

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani faces intense public backlash, and Staten Island crowd members booed him at a recent event. National correspondent Bryan Llenas reports on independent estimates showing Mamdani's free universal child care program could cost $9.3 billion dollars, fifty-five percent more than claimed.

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani approved roughly $850,000 in pay raises for dozens of City Hall aides during his first year in office, according to municipal payroll records reviewed by the New York Post.

The records show the raises were distributed to at least 46 staffers since Mamdani took office in January, bringing total compensation increases near $1 million.

The Post counted 21 new hires who received raises totaling $450,000 before their first anniversary on the job, more than twice the combined number of first-year employees who got raises under Eric Adams and Bill de Blasio.

On Jan. 28, five City Hall employees were granted salary increases totaling $95,000 on the same day Mamdani announced New York City faced an estimated $12 billion budget deficit, the records show. The mayor’s office later lowered that projected deficit to approximately $5.4 billion.

The mayor’s office on Nov. 12 confirmed the city’s fiscal 2027 budget rose $3.3 billion from the budget adopted in June, reaching $129.2 billion.

The payroll disclosures detail significant salary adjustments across several executive staff positions.

The salary increases come as Mamdani, who took office Jan. 1 as the city's first self-described socialist mayor, faces fiscal constraints and administrative hurdles on core campaign pledges.

The administration has also faced capacity limits with its universal childcare initiative. A pilot program aimed at 2-year-olds launched with 2,000 available seats but drew more than 5,700 applicants, leaving roughly 3,700 children on waitlists. #5844. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5838. Group of Senate Democrats push to add freshman to leadership

U.S. Senate Majority Leader Charles Schumer (D-NY), center, is seen here meeting with newly elected Democratic Senators, (L-R) Senator-elect Andy Kim (D-NJ), Senator-elect Ruben Gallego (D-AZ), Senator-elect Angela Alsobrooks (D-MD) Senator-elect Adam Schiff (D-CA) Senator-elect Lisa Blunt Rochester (D-DE) and Senator-elect Elissa Slotkin (D-MI) at the U.S. Capitol Building on November 12, 2024. That group of senators is pushing to add an incoming freshman senator to the leadership ranks. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images/Getty Images North America hide caption

A group of Senate Democrats elected in 2024 sent a letter to colleagues Tuesday proposing to add an incoming freshman to the leadership ranks.

"The Freshman Representative to Leadership would be elected by members of the incoming freshman class and confirmed by the Conference," the letter said. "We intend to request a vote on this proposal."

The letter, first reported by Politico, is signed by California Sen. Adam Schiff, Maryland Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, Michigan Sen. Elissa Slotkin, Arizona Sen. Ruben Gallego, Delaware Sen. Lisa Blunt Rochester, and New Jersey Sen. Andy Kim.

The letter nods to the diverse class of incoming freshmen who helped catapult the party to controlling both chambers of Congress.

"These freshmen have their fingers on the pulse of long-time Democratic voters, those who voted for Democrats for the first time this past Election Day, and everyone in-between," the letter reads. "The deserve a seat at the table."

Senate Democrats have been signaling a desire to uplift new voices in the party.

"On election night, people voted very clearly for a new generation of leadership. That was very, very clear," Slotkin said during remarks at a conference of Democrats after the midterms.

New Jersey Sen. Cory Booker told MS NOW that leadership extends beyond the top post.

"This is a time for new leadership," Booker said, mentioning wanting to see more of incoming Sens. Annie Andrews of South Carolina and James Talarico of Texas. "I want to see them out front, I want to see them taking issues, I want to see them running on the field."

The proposed rule change comes ahead of the leadership election next week. Maryland Sen. Chris Van Hollen is challenging Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., for the majority leader.

Senate leadership is comprised not only of the top position of Majority leader but of additional conference officers. Both Van Hollen and Schumer support the idea of adding a position for a freshman to that leadership group.

"Leader Schumer was already planning to do this and is incredibly supportive of the idea," a spokesperson told NPR.

On Tuesday, Van Hollen posted to social media: "There should be a freshman senator on the Senate leadership team. As I've said, we need a new way to organize so we can deliver for the American people."

#5837. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.
